Let  $p_1, p_2, \ldots, p_q$  be non-negative weights such that  $\sum_{i} p_i = 1$  and  $p = \min_{i \leqslant q} p_i$ . Then, the mixture  $\sum_{i} p_i \mathcal{D}_i$  is  $t$ -certifiably  $C/p$ -subgaussian.

Certifiable Anti-Concentration. The first is certifiable anti-concentration — an SoS formulation of classical anti-concentration inequalities — that was introduced in [KKK19, RY20a].

In order to formulate certifiable anti-concentration, we start with a univariate even polynomial  $p$  that serves as a uniform approximation to the delta function at 0 in an interval around 0. Such polynomials are constructed in [KKK19, RY20a] (see also [DGJ+10]). Let  $q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v)$  be a multivariate (in  $v$ ) polynomial defined by  $q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v) = (v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{2s}p_{\delta,\Sigma}\left(\frac{\langle x,v\rangle}{\sqrt{v^{\top}\Sigma v}}\right)$ . Since  $p_{\delta,\Sigma}$  is an even polynomial,  $q_{\delta,\Sigma}$  is a polynomial in  $v$ .

Definition 2.28 (Certifiable Anti-Concentration). A mean-0 distribution  $D$  with covariance  $\Sigma$  is 2s-certifiably  $(\delta, C\delta)$ -anti-concentrated if for  $q_{\delta, \Sigma}(x, v)$  defined above, there exists a degree-2s sum-of-squares proof of the following two unconstrained polynomial inequalities in indeterminate  $v$ :

$$
\left\{\langle x, v \rangle^ {2 s} + \delta^ {2 s} q _ {\delta , \Sigma} (x, v) ^ {2} \geqslant \delta^ {2 s} \left(v ^ {\top} \Sigma v\right) ^ {2 s} \right\}, \left\{\underset {x \sim D} {\mathbb {E}} q _ {\delta , \Sigma} (x, v) ^ {2} \leqslant C \delta \left(v ^ {\top} \Sigma v\right) ^ {2 s} \right\}.
$$

An isotropic subset  $X \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d$  is 2s-certifiably  $(\delta, C\delta)$ -anti-concentrated if the uniform distribution on  $X$  is 2s-certifiably  $(\delta, C\delta)$ -anti-concentrated.

Remark 2.29. The function  $s(\delta)$  can be taken to be  $O\left(\frac{1}{\delta^2}\right)$  for standard Gaussian distribution and the uniform distribution on the unit sphere (see [KKK19] and [BK20a]).

Certifiable Hypercontractivity. Next, we define certifiable hypercontractivity of degree-2 polynomials that formulates (within SoS) the fact that higher moments of degree-2 polynomials of distributions (such as Gaussians) can be bounded in terms of appropriate powers of their 2nd moment.

Definition 2.30 (Certifiable Hypercontractivity). An isotropic distribution  $\mathcal{D}$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  is said to be  $h$ -certifiably  $C$ -hypercontractive if there is a degree- $h$  sum-of-squares proof of the following unconstrained polynomial inequality in  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate  $Q$ :

$$
\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} (x ^ {\top} Q x) ^ {h} \leqslant (C h) ^ {h} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} (x ^ {\top} Q x) ^ {2}\right) ^ {h / 2}.
$$

A set of points  $X \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d$  is said to be  $C$ -certifiably hypercontractive if the uniform distribution on  $X$  is  $h$ -certifiably  $C$ -hypercontractive.

Hypercontractivity is an important notion in high-dimensional probability and analysis on product spaces [O'D14]. Kauers, O'Donnell, Tan and Zhou [KOTZ14] showed certifiable hypercontractivity of Gaussians and more generally product distributions with subgaussian marginals. Certifiable hypercontractivity strictly generalizes the better known certifiable subgaussianity property (studied first in [KS17b]) that controls higher moments of linear polynomials.

Observe that the definition above is affine invariant. In particular, we immediately obtain:

Fact 2.31. Given  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ , if a random variable  $x$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  has  $t$ -certifiable C-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials, then so does  $Ax$  for any  $A \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ .

As observed in [KS17b], the Gaussian distribution is  $t$ -certifiably 1-subgaussian and  $t$ -certifiable 1-hypercontractive for every  $t$ . Next, we establish certifiable hypercontractivity for mixtures of Gaussians. We defer the proofs to Appendix A.

Lemma 2.32 (Shifts Cannot Decrease Variance). Let  $\mathcal{D}$  be a distribution on  $\mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $Q$  be a  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate, and  $C$  be a scalar-valued indeterminate. Then, we have that

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q , C}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (Q (x) - C) ^ {2} \right]\right\}.
$$

Lemma 2.33 (Shifts of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions). Let  $x$  be a mean-0 random variable with distribution  $\mathcal{D}$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  with t-certifiably C-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials. Then, for any fixed constant vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^d$ , the random variable  $x + c$  also has t-certifiable 4C-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials.

Lemma 2.34 (Mixtures of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions). Let  $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \ldots, \mathcal{D}_k$  have  $t$ -certifiable  $C$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials on  $\mathbb{R}^d$ , for some fixed constant  $C$ . Then, any mixture  $\mathcal{D} = \sum_{i} w_i \mathcal{D}_i$  also has  $t$ -certifiably  $(C / \alpha)$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials for  $\alpha = \min_{i \leqslant k, w_i > 0} w_i$ .

Corollary 2.35 (Certifiable Hypercontractivity of Mixtures of  $k$  Gaussians). Let  $\mathcal{M}$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians  $\sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$  with weights  $w_{i} \geqslant \alpha$  for every  $i \in [k]$ . Then, for all  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ ,  $\mathcal{D}$  has  $t$ -certifiably  $4 / \alpha$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials.

We will use the following robust mean estimation algorithm for bounded covariance distributions [DKK+17]:

Fact 2.36 (Robust Mean Estimation for Bounded Covariance Distributions). There is a poly(n) time algorithm that takes input an  $\varepsilon$ -corruption  $Y$  of a collection of  $n$  points  $X \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d$ , and outputs an estimate  $\hat{\mu}$  satisfying  $\left\| \mathbb{E}_{x \sim_u X} x - \hat{\mu} \right\|_2 \leqslant O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}) \left\| \mathbb{E}_{x \sim_u X} (x - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim_u X} x) (x - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim_u X} x)^\top \right\|_2$ .

We will use the following robust covariance estimation algorithm from [KS17b]:

Fact 2.37 (Robust Covariance Estimation, [KS17b]). For every  $C > 0$ ,  $\varepsilon > 0$  and even  $k \in \mathbb{N}$  such that  $C k \varepsilon^{1 - 2 / k} \leqslant c$  for some small enough absolute constant  $c$ , there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that given an (corrupted) sample  $S$  outputs an estimate of the covariance  $\hat{\Sigma} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$  with the following guarantee: there exists  $n_0 \geqslant (C + d)^{O(k)} / \varepsilon$  such that if  $S$  is an  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted sample with size  $|S| \geqslant n_0$  of a  $k$ -certifiably  $C$ -subgaussian distribution  $D$  over  $\mathbb{R}^d$  with mean  $\mu \in \mathbb{R}^d$  and covariance  $\Sigma \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ , then with high probability:

$$
(1 - \delta) \Sigma \geq \hat {\Sigma} \succeq (1 + \delta) \Sigma
$$

for  $\delta \leqslant O(Ck)\varepsilon^{1 - 2 / k}$

We will also require the following robust estimation algorithm with Frobenius distance guarantees proven for certifiably hypercontractive distributions in [BK20b]. Since we obtain estimates to the true covariance in Lowner ordering, we can obtain the subspace spanned by the inliers exactly, project on to this subspace and apply Theorem 7.1 in [BK20b].

Fact 2.38 (Robust Mean and Covariance Estimation for Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions, Theorem 7.1 in [BK20b]). Given  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ , and  $\varepsilon > 0$  sufficiently small so that  $Ct\varepsilon^{1 - 4 / t} \ll 1^6$ , for some absolute constant  $C > 0$ . Then, there is an algorithm that takes input  $Y$ , an  $\varepsilon$ -corruption of a sample  $X$  of size  $n$  with mean  $\mu_*$ , covariance  $\Sigma_*$ , and  $2t$ -certifiably  $C$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials, runs in time  $n^{O(t)}$ , and outputs an estimate  $\hat{\mu}$  and  $\hat{\Sigma}$  satisfying:

1.  $\left\| \Sigma_{*}^{+ / 2}(\mu_{*} - \hat{\mu})\right\|_{2}\leqslant O(Ct)^{1 / 2}\varepsilon^{1 - 1 / t},$  
2.  $(1 - \eta)\Sigma_{*}\preceq \hat{\Sigma}\preceq (1 + \eta)\Sigma_{*}$  for  $\eta \leqslant O(Ck)\varepsilon^{1 - 2 / t}$ , and,  
3.  $\left\| \Sigma_{*}^{\dagger /2}\left(\hat{\Sigma} -\Sigma_{*}\right)\Sigma_{*}^{\dagger /2}\right\| _F\leqslant (C't)O(\varepsilon^{1 - 1 / t}),$

where  $C' = \max \{C, B\}$  for the smallest possible  $B > 0$  such that for  $d \times d$ -matrix-valued indeterminate  $Q$ ,

$$
\left. \left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\mathbb {E} _ {\mathcal {D}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \mathbb {E} _ {\mathcal {D}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \leqslant B \left\| \Sigma_ {*} ^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma_ {*} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right. \right.
$$

The last line in the above fact asserts a bound (along with a degree 2 SoS proof) on the variance of degree 2 polynomials in terms of the Frobenius norm of its coefficient matrix. In the next few claims, we verify this property via elementary arguments for the two classes of distributions relevant to

this paper. We note that whenever a distribution satisfies the bounded variance property (without an SoS proof), it also satisfies the property via a degree 2 SoS proof using Lemma 2.23. Thus, asking for an SoS proof of degree 2 in this context poses no additional restrictions on the distribution. Nevertheless, we provide explicit and direct SoS proofs in the following.

We first note that this property of having certifiable bounded variance is closed under linear transformations.

Lemma 2.39 (Linear Transformations of Certifiably Bounded-Variance Distributions). For  $d \in \mathbb{N}$ , let  $x$  be a random variable with distribution  $\mathcal{D}$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  such that for  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate  $Q$ ,  $\left|\frac{Q}{2}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}}(x^\top Qx - \mathbb{E}_\mathcal{D}x^\top Qx)\right|^2 \leqslant \left\|\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\|_F^2\right\}$ . Let  $A$  be an arbitrary  $d \times d$  matrix and let  $x' = Ax$  be the random variable with covariance  $\Sigma' = AA^\top$ . Then, we have that

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x ^ {\prime} \sim \mathcal {D} ^ {\prime}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\prime^ {\top}} Q x ^ {\prime} - \underset {\mathcal {D} ^ {\prime}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\prime^ {\top}} Q x ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \leqslant \left\| \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Lemma 2.40 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Standard Gaussians). We have that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \leqslant 3 \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right.
$$

Remark 2.41. As is easy to verify, the same proof more generally holds for any distribution that has the same first four moments as the zero-mean Gaussian distribution.

As an immediate corollary of the previous two lemmas, we have:

Corollary 2.42 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Zero-Mean, Arbitrary Covariance Gaussians) For any  $0 \preceq \Sigma$ , we have that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {\mathcal {N} (0, \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {N} (0, \Sigma)} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \leqslant 3 \left\| \Sigma^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right.
$$

We next prove that the same property holds for mixtures of Gaussians satisfying certain conditions.

Lemma 2.43 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Mixtures). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{D}_{i}$  be a  $k$ -mixture of distributions  $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \ldots, \mathcal{D}_k$  with means  $\mu_{i}$  and covariances  $\Sigma_{i}$ . Let  $\mu = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mu_{i}$  be the mean of  $\mathcal{M}$ . Suppose that each of  $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \ldots, \mathcal{D}_k$  have certifiably  $C$ -bounded-variance i.e. for  $Q$ : a symmetric  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate.

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x ^ {\prime} \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\prime^ {\top}} Q x ^ {\prime} - \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\prime^ {\top}} Q x ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \leqslant C \left\| \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Further, suppose that for some  $H > 1$ ,  $\left\| \mu_i - \mu \right\|_2^2$ ,  $\left\| \Sigma_i - I \right\|_F \leqslant H$  for every  $1 \leqslant i \leqslant k$ . Then, we have that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} [ x ^ {\top} Q x ]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant 1 0 0 C H ^ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right.
$$

As an immediate corollary of Lemma 2.39 and Lemma 2.43, we obtain:

Lemma 2.44 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Mixtures of Gaussians). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians with  $w_{i}\geqslant \alpha$ , mean  $\mu = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}$  and covariance  $\Sigma = \sum_{i}w_{i}((\mu_{i} - \mu)(\mu_{i} - \mu)^{\top} + \Sigma_{i})$ . Suppose that for some  $H > 1$ ,  $\left\| \Sigma^{\dagger /2}(\Sigma_i - \Sigma)\Sigma^{\dagger /2}\right\| _F\leqslant H$  for every  $1\leqslant i\leqslant k$ . Let  $Q$  be a symmetric  $d\times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate. Then for  $H^{\prime} = \max \{H,1 / \alpha \}$ ,

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant 1 0 0 H ^ {\prime 2} \left\| \Sigma^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Analytic Properties are Inherited by Samples. The following lemma can be proven via similar, standard techniques as in several prior works [KS17b, KKK19, BK20a, BK20b].

Fact 2.45. Let  $D$  be a distribution on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  with mean  $\mu$  and covariance  $\Sigma$ . Let  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ . Let  $X$  be a sample from  $D$  such that,  $\left\| \frac{1}{|X|} \sum_{x \in X} (1, \bar{x})^{\otimes t} - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim D}(1, \bar{x})^{\otimes t} \right\|_F \leqslant d^{-O(t)}$ . Here,  $\bar{x} = \Sigma^{\dagger/2}(x - \mu_i)$ . Then,

1. If  $D$  is 2t-certifiably  $C$ -subgaussian, then the uniform distribution on  $X$  is t-certifiably 2C-subgaussian.  
2. If  $D$  has 2t-certifiably  $C$ -hypercontractive degree 2 polynomials, then the uniform distribution on  $X$  has t-certifiably 2C-hypercontractive degree 2 polynomials.  
3. If  $D$  is 2t-certifiably  $C\delta$ -anti-concentrated, then the uniform distribution on  $X$  is t-certifiably 2Cδ-anti-concentrated.  
4. If  $\left|\frac{Q}{2}\right| \left\{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}}(x^{\top} Q x - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}} x^{\top} Q x)^{2} \leqslant C \| Q \|_{F}^{2}\right\}$ , then, for the uniform distribution  $\mathcal{D}_X$  on  $X$ ,  $\left|\frac{Q}{2}\right| \left\{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}_X}(x^{\top} Q x - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}_X} x^{\top} Q x)^{2} \leqslant 2C \| Q \|_{F}^{2}\right\}$ .

# 2.4 Deterministic Conditions on the Uncorrupted Samples

In this section, we describe the set of deterministic conditions on the set of uncorrupted samples, under which our algorithms succeed. We will require the following definition.

Definition 2.46. Fix  $0 < \varepsilon < 1/2$ . We say that a multiset  $Y$  of points in  $\mathbb{R}^d$  is an  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted version (or an  $\varepsilon$ -corruption) of a multiset  $X$  of points in  $\mathbb{R}^d$  if  $|X \cap Y| \geqslant \max \{(1 - \varepsilon)|X|, (1 - \varepsilon)|Y|\}$ .

Throughout this paper and unless otherwise specified, we will use  $X$  to denote a multiset of i.i.d. samples from the target  $k$ -mixture  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$ , where  $G_i = \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$ . We will use  $X_i$  for the subset of points in  $X$  drawn from  $G_i$ , i.e.,  $X = \cup_{i=1}^{k} X_i$ .

We will use  $Y$  to denote an  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted version of  $X$ , as per Definition 2.46. In this strong contamination model, the adversary can see the clean samples from  $X$  before they decide on the  $\varepsilon$ -corruption  $Y$ . The strong contamination model is known to subsume the total variation contamination of Definition 1.2 (see, e.g., Section 2 of [DKK+16]). We note that our robust learning algorithm succeeds in this stronger contamination model, with the additional requirement that we can obtain two sets of independent  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted samples from  $\mathcal{M}$ . (The second set is needed to run a hypothesis testing routine after we obtain a small list of candidate hypotheses.)

Our algorithm works for any finite set of points in  $\mathbb{R}^d$  that satisfies a natural set of deterministic conditions. As we will show later in this section, these deterministic conditions are satisfied with high probability by a sufficiently large set of i.i.d. samples from any  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians.

Condition 2.47 (Good Samples). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians in  $\mathbb{R}^d$ . Let  $X$  be a set of  $n$  points in  $\mathbb{R}^d$ . We say that  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$  if there is a partition of  $X$  as  $X_1 \cup X_2 \cup \ldots \cup X_k$  such that:

1. For all  $i \in [k]$  with  $w_i \geqslant \gamma$ , any positive integer  $m \leqslant t$ , and any  $v \in \mathbb{R}^d$ ,

$$
\left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {x \in X _ {i}} \langle v, x - \mu_ {i} \rangle^ {m} - w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu_ {i}, \Sigma_ {i})} {\mathbb {E}} [ \langle v, x - \mu_ {i} \rangle^ {m} ] \right| \leqslant w _ {i} \gamma m! (v ^ {T} \Sigma_ {i} v) ^ {m / 2}.
$$

2. For all  $i\in [k]$  and any halfspace  $H\subset \mathbb{R}^d$  , we have that  $\left|\left|X_i\cap H\right| / n - w_i\mathbf{P}_{x\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_i,\Sigma_i)}[x\in H]\right|\leqslant \gamma .$

We will also need the following consequences of Condition 2.47. The first one is immediate.

Lemma 2.48. Condition 2.47 is invariant under affine transformations. In particular, if  $A(x): \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^{d'}$  is an affine transformation, and if  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$ , then  $A(X)$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $A(\mathcal{M})$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$ .

We note that the first part of Condition 2.47 implies that higher moment tensors are close in Frobenius distance.

Lemma 2.49. If  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$ , then if  $w_{i} \geqslant \gamma$  for all  $i \in [k]$ , and if for some  $B \geqslant 0$  we have that  $\left\| \mu_{i} \right\|_{2}^{2}$ ,  $\| \Sigma_{i} \|_{\mathrm{op}} \leqslant B$  for all  $i \in [k]$ , then for all  $m \leqslant t$ , we have that:

$$
\left\| \mathop {\mathbb {E}} _ {x \in_ {U} X} [ x ^ {\otimes m} ] - \mathop {\mathbb {E}} _ {x \sim M} [ x ^ {\otimes m} ] \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant \gamma^ {2} m ^ {O (m)} B ^ {m} d ^ {m}.
$$

We note that Condition 2.47 also behaves well with respect to taking submixtures.

Lemma 2.50. Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$ . Let  $S \subset [k]$  with  $\sum_{i \in S} w_{i} = w$ , and let  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i \in S} (w_{i} / w) \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$ . Then if  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$  for some  $\gamma < 1/(2k)$  with the corresponding partition being  $X = X_{1} \cup X_{2} \cup \ldots \cup X_{k}$ , then  $X' = \bigcup_{i \in S} X_{i}$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}'$  with parameters  $(O(k\gamma / w), t)$ .

Finally, we show that given sufficiently many i.i.d. samples from a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians, Condition 2.47 holds with high probability.

Lemma 2.51. Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  and let  $n$  be an integer at least  $kt^{Ct}d^t / \gamma^3$ , for a sufficiently large universal constant  $C > 0$ , some  $\gamma > 0$ , and some  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ . If  $X$  consists of  $n$  i.i.d. samples from  $\mathcal{M}$ , then  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$  with high probability.

The proofs of the preceding lemmas can be found in Appendix A.

# 2.5 Hypothesis Selection

Our algorithm will require a procedure to select a hypothesis from a list of candidates that contains an accurate hypothesis. A number of such procedures are known in the literature (see, e.g., [Yat85, DL01, DDS12, DK14, DDS15, DKK $^{+}$ 16]). Here we will use the following variant from [Kan20], showing that we can efficiently perform a hypothesis selection (tournament) step with access to  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted samples.

Fact 2.52 (Robust Tournament, [Kan20]). Let  $X$  be an unknown distribution,  $\eta \in (0,1)$ , and let  $H_1, \ldots, H_n$  be distributions with explicitly computable probability density functions that can be efficiently sampled from. Assume furthermore that  $\min_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant n} (d_{\mathsf{TV}}(X, H_i)) \leqslant \eta$ . Then there exists an efficient algorithm that given access to  $O(\log(n) / \eta^2)$ $\varepsilon$ -corrupted samples from  $X$ , where  $\varepsilon \leqslant \eta$ , along with  $H_1, \ldots, H_n$ , computes an  $m \in [n]$  such that with high probability we have that  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(X, H_m) = O(\eta)$ .

# 3 List-Recovery of Parameters via Tensor Decomposition

In this section, we give an algorithm that takes samples from a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians, whose component means and covariances are not too far from each other in natural norms, and outputs a dimension-independent size list of candidate  $k$ -tuples of parameters (i.e., means and covariances) one of which is guaranteed to be close to the true target  $k$ -tuple of parameters. Our approach involves a new tensor decomposition procedure that works in the absence of any non-degeneracy conditions on the components.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 3.1 (Recovering Candidate Parameters when Component Covariances are close in Frobenius Distance). Fix any  $\alpha >\varepsilon >0,\Delta >0$  . There is an algorithm that takes input  $X$  , a sample from a  $k$  -mixture of Gaussians  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$  satisfying Condition 2.47 with parameters  $\gamma = \varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}$  for  $C$  a sufficiently large universal constant, and  $t = 8k$  , and let  $Y$  be an  $\varepsilon$  -corruption of  $X$  . If  $w_{i}\geqslant \alpha$ $\left\| \mu_i\right\| _2\leqslant \frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}$  and  $\| \Sigma_i - I\| _F\leqslant \Delta$  for every  $i\in [k]$  , then, given  $k,Y$  and  $\varepsilon$  , the algorithm outputs a list  $L$  of at most  $\exp \left(\log (1 / \varepsilon)(k + 1 / \alpha +\Delta)^{O(k)} / \eta^2\right)$  candidate hypotheses (component means and covariances), such that with probability at least 0.99 there exist  $\{\hat{\mu}_i,\hat{\Sigma}_i\}_{i\in [k]}\subseteq L$  satisfying  $\left\| \mu_i - \hat{\mu}_i\right\| _2\leqslant O\Big(\frac{\Delta^{1 / 2}}{\alpha}\Big)\eta^{G(k)}$  and  $\left\| \Sigma_i - \hat{\Sigma}_i\right\| _F\leqslant O\bigl (k^4\bigr)\frac{\Delta^{1 / 2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}$  for all  $i\in [k]$  . Here,  $\eta = (2k)^{4k}O(1 / \alpha +\Delta)^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ $G(k) = \frac{1}{C^{k + 1}(k + 1)!}$  The running time of the algorithm is poly(|L|, |Y|,  $d^{k}$

In the body of this section, we establish Theorem 3.1. The structure of this section is as follows: In Section 3.1, we describe our algorithm, which is then analyzed in Sections 3.2-3.6.

# 3.1 List-Decodable Tensor Decomposition Algorithm

In this section, we describe our tensor decomposition algorithm, which is given in pseudocode below (Algorithm 3.2).

Algorithm 3.2 (List-Recovery of Candidate Parameters via Tensor Decomposition).

Input: An  $\varepsilon$ -corruption  $Y$  of a sample  $X$  from a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$ .

Requirements: The guarantees of the algorithm hold if the mixture parameters and the sample  $X$  satisfy:

1.  $w_{i}\geqslant \alpha$  forall  $i\in [k],$  
2.  $\left\| \mu_i\right\| _2\leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$  for all  $i\in [k]$  
3.  $\| \Sigma_{i} - I \|_{F} \leqslant \Delta$  for all  $i \in [k]$ .  
4.  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$ , where  $\gamma = \varepsilon d^{-8k} k^{-Ck}$ , for  $C$  a sufficiently large universal constant, and  $t = 8k$ .

Parameters:  $\eta = (2k)^{4k}(Ck(1 / \alpha +\Delta))^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon},D = C(k^4 /(\alpha \sqrt{\eta})),\delta = 2\eta^{1 / (C^{k + 1}(k + 1)!)},\ell ' =$ $100\log k\left(\eta /\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4} + 1 / \alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}$  , for some sufficiently large absolute constant  $C > 0,\lambda = 4\eta$ $\phi = 10(1 + \Delta^2) / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^5).$

Output: A list  $L$  of hypotheses such that there exists at least one,  $\{\hat{\mu}_i,\hat{\Sigma}_i\}_{i\leqslant k}\in L$  satisfying:  $\left\| \mu_i - \hat{\mu}_i\right\| _2\leqslant O\Big(\frac{\Delta^{1 / 2}}{\alpha}\Big)\eta^{G(k)}$  and  $\left\| \Sigma_{i} - \hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\|_{F}\leqslant O\bigl (k^{4}\bigr)\frac{\Delta^{1 / 2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}$  where  $G(k) = \frac{1}{C^{k + 1}(k + 1)!}$ .

# Operation:

1. Robust Estimation of Hermite Tensors: For  $m \in [4k]$ , compute  $\hat{T}_m$  such that  $\max_{m \in [4k]} \left\| \hat{T}_m - \mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})] \right\|_F \leqslant \eta$  using the robust mean estimation algorithm in Fact 2.36.  
2. Random Collapsing of Two Modes of  $\hat{T}_4$ : Let  $L'$  be an empty list. Repeat  $\ell'$  times: For  $j \in [4k]$ , choose independent standard Gaussians in  $\mathbb{R}^d$ , denoted by  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I)$ , and uniform draws  $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_t$  from  $[-D, D]$ . Let  $\hat{S}$  be a  $d \times d$  matrix such that for all  $r, s \in [d]$ ,  $\hat{S}(r, s) = \sum_{j \in [4k]} a_j \hat{T}_4(r, s, x^{(j)}, y^{(j)}) = \sum_{j \in [4k]} a_j \sum_{g, h \in [d]} \hat{T}_4(r, s, g, h) x^{(j)}(g) y^{(j)}(h)$ . Add  $\hat{S}$  to the list  $L'$ .  
3. Construct Low-Dimensional Subspace for Exhaustive Search: Let  $V$  be the span of all singular vectors of the natural  $d \times d^{m-1}$  flattening of  $\hat{T}_m$  with singular values  $\geqslant \lambda$  for  $m \leqslant 4k$ . For each  $\hat{S} \in L'$ , let  $V_{\hat{S}}'$  be the span of  $V$  plus all the singular vectors of  $\hat{S}$  with singular value larger than  $\delta^{1/4}$ .  
4. Enumerating Candidates in  $V_{\hat{S}}'$ : Initialize  $L$  to be the empty list. For each  $\hat{S} \in L'$ , let  $V_{\delta^{1/4}}$  be a  $\delta^{1/4}$ -cover of vectors in  $V_{\hat{S}}'$  with  $\ell_2$ -norm at most  $2/\sqrt{\alpha}$ . Enumerate over vectors  $\hat{\mu}$  in  $V_{\delta^{1/4}}$ . Let  $k' = Ck^2$  and let  $C_{\delta^{1/4}}$  be a  $\delta^{1/4}$ -cover of the interval  $[-\phi, \phi]^{k'}$ . For  $\{\tau_j\}_{j \in [k']} \in C_{\delta^{1/4}}$  and for all  $\{v_j\}_{j \in [k']} \in V_{\delta^{1/4}}$ , let  $\hat{Q} = \sum_{j \in [k']} \tau_j v_j v_j^\top$ . Add  $\{\hat{\mu}, I + \hat{S} + \hat{Q}\}$  to  $L$ .

# 3.2 Analysis of Algorithm

We analyze the three main steps of Algorithm 3.2 in the following lemmas. We will prove the following three propositions in the subsequent subsections that analyze Steps 1, 2 and 3 of Algorithm 3.2. For Step 1, we show that when  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47, the empirical estimates of the moment tensors obtained by applying the robust mean estimation algorithm to  $X$  are sufficiently close to the moment tensors of the input mixture  $\mathcal{M}$ .

Proposition 3.3 (Robustly Estimating Hermite Polynomial Tensors). For any integer  $m \leqslant 4k$ , and  $\Delta \in \mathbb{R}_{+}$ , there exists an algorithm with running time  $\mathrm{poly}_m(d / \varepsilon)$  that takes an  $\varepsilon$ -corruption  $Y$  of  $X$ , a set satisfying Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  with parameters  $\gamma = \varepsilon d^{-m} m^{-Cm}$ , for  $C$  sufficiently large constant, and  $t = 2m$ . If  $w_i \geqslant \alpha$ ,  $\| \mu_i \|_2 \leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$ , and  $\| \Sigma_i - I \|_F \leqslant \Delta$  for each  $i \in [k]$ , then the algorithm outputs a tensor  $\hat{T}_m$  such that  $\left\| \hat{T}_m - \mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})] \right\|_F \leqslant \eta$ , for  $\eta = O(m(1 + 1 / \alpha + \Delta))^m \sqrt{\varepsilon}$ .

The proof of Proposition 3.3 is deferred to Section 3.3.

Next, we analyze Step 2 of the algorithm and prove that, with non-negligible probability, randomly collapsing two modes of  $\hat{T}_4$  yields a matrix  $\hat{S}$  such that  $\hat{S} - (\Sigma_i - I) = P_i + Q_i$ , where  $P_i$  has small Frobenius norm and  $Q_i$  is a rank- $\mathcal{O}(k^2)$  matrix.

Proposition 3.4 (Tensor Decomposition up to Low-Rank Error). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians satisfying  $w_i \geqslant \alpha$ ,  $\left\| \mu_i \right\|_2 \leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$ , and  $\left\| \Sigma_i - I \right\|_F \leqslant \Delta$  for each  $i \in [k]$ . For  $0 < \eta < 1$ , let  $\hat{T}_4$  be a tensor such that  $\left\| \mathbb{E}[h_4(\mathcal{M})] - \hat{T}_4 \right\|_F \leqslant \eta$ , and let  $D$  be a sufficiently large constant multiple of  $k^4 / (\alpha \sqrt{\eta})$ . For all  $j \in [4k]$ , let  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I)$  be independent and  $a_j \sim \mathcal{U}[-D, D]$ , where  $\mathcal{U}[-D, D]$  is the uniform distribution over the interval  $[-D, D]$ , and let  $\hat{S} = \sum_{j \in [4k]} a_j \hat{T}_4(\cdot, \cdot, x^{(j)}, y^{(j)})$ . Then, for each  $i \in [k]$ , with probability at least  $(\eta / (k^5 (\Delta^4 + 1 / \alpha^4)))^{4k}$ , over the choice of  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)}$  and  $a_j$ , we have that  $\hat{S} - (\Sigma_i - I) = P_i + Q_i$ , where  $\|P_i\|_F = O\left(\sqrt{\eta / \alpha}\right)$ ,  $\|Q_i\|_F = O\left(\frac{1 + \Delta^2}{\sqrt{\eta} \alpha^3}\right)$  and  $\mathrm{rank}(Q_i) = O(k^2)$ .

The proof of Proposition 3.4 is given in Section 3.4.

Finally, in Step 3, for any  $\hat{S}$  such that  $\hat{S} - (\Sigma_i - I) = P_i + Q_i$ , where  $P_i$  has small Frobenius norm and  $Q_i$  is a rank  $O(k^2)$  matrix, we find a low-dimensional subspace  $V'$  such that the range space of  $Q_i$  is approximately contained in  $V'$ . We will use  $V'$  to exhaustively search for  $O(k^2)$  rank matrices to find candidates for  $Q_i$ .

Proposition 3.5 (Low-Dimensional Subspace  $V'$  for Exhaustive Search). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians satisfying  $w_i \geqslant \alpha$ ,  $\left\| \mu_i \right\|_2 \leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}$ , and  $\left\| \Sigma_i - I \right\|_F \leqslant \Delta$  for each  $i \in [k]$ . Let  $\left\| \hat{T}_m - \mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})] \right\|_F \leqslant \eta$ , for each  $1 \leqslant m \leqslant 4k$ , and some  $\eta > 0$ . Let  $V$  be the span of all the left singular vectors of the  $d \times d^{m-1}$  matrix obtained by the natural flattening of  $\hat{T}_m$  with singular values at least  $2\eta$ . For each  $1 \leqslant i \leqslant k$ , let  $S_i = \Sigma_i - I$  and  $\hat{S}_i$  be a  $d \times d$  matrix such that  $\hat{S}_i - S_i = P_i + Q_i$ , where  $\|P_i\|_F \leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)$ .  $Q_i$  has rank  $O(k^2)$ , and  $\|Q_i\|_F \leqslant O\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^3}\right)$ . Let  $V'$  be the span of  $V$  plus all singular vectors of  $\hat{S}_i$  of singular values at least  $\delta$  for all  $i$ . Then, for  $\delta = 2\eta^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$  with a sufficiently large constant  $C > 0$ , we have that

1.  $\dim(V') \leqslant \left( \mathcal{O}(k(1 + 1 / \alpha + \Delta))^{4k + 5} \right) / \eta^2$ .

2. There is a vector  $\mu_i' \in V'$  such that  $\left\| \mu_i - \mu_i' \right\|_2^2 \leqslant \frac{20}{\alpha^2} \sqrt{\delta} \Delta$ .  
3. There are  $q = O(k^2)$  unit vectors  $v_1, v_2, \ldots, v_q \in V'$  and scalars  $\tau_1, \tau_2, \ldots, \tau_q \in \left[-10(1 + \Delta^2) / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^5), 10(1 + \Delta^2) / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^5)\right]$  such that  $\left\| Q_i - \sum_{i=1}^{q} \tau_i v_i v_i^\top \right\|_F \leqslant O\left(\frac{k^2}{\alpha} \delta^{1/4} \Delta^{1/2}\right)$ .

The proof of Proposition 3.5 is given in Section 3.5.

We can now use these propositions to complete the proof of Theorem 3.1.

Proof of Theorem 3.1. Using Proposition 3.3, Step 1 of the algorithm outputs estimates  $\hat{T}_i$  for  $i\in [4k]$  such that  $\max_{m\in [4k]}\left\| \hat{T}_m - \mathbb{E}h_m(\mathcal{M})\right\| _F\leqslant \eta$ . Next, by the standard coupon collector analysis, using Proposition 3.4 and repeating Step 2 of the algorithm  $\ell^{\prime} = 100\log k\left(\eta /\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4} + 1 / \alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}$  times, guarantees that with probability at least  $1 - 1 / (100k)^{100}$ , for every  $1\leqslant i\leqslant k$ , there are  $\hat{S}_i\in L$  such that  $\hat{S}_i - (\Sigma_i - I) = P_i + Q_i$  for  $P_{i},Q_{i}$  satisfying  $\| P_i\| _F\leqslant \sqrt{\eta / \alpha}$ ,  $\| Q_i\| _F\leqslant \frac{1 + \Delta^2}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^5}$  and  $Q_{i}$  has rank  $O(k^{2})$ .

Next, Proposition 3.5 implies that for every such  $\hat{S}_i\in L'$ , we can construct a subspace  $V^{\prime} = V_{\hat{S}_i}^{\prime}$  of dimension  $O\big((k(1 + 1 / \alpha +\Delta))^{4k + 5} / \eta^2\big)$  such that  $V^{\prime}$  contains  $\mu_i^\prime$  that satisfies  $\left\| \mu_i - \mu_i^\prime \right\| _2^2\leqslant \frac{\Delta}{\alpha^2}\cdot \sqrt{\delta},$  and there is a rank  $O(k^2)$  matrix  $\hat{Q}_i$  with range space contained in  $V^{\prime}$  such that  $\left\| Q_i - \hat{Q}_i\right\| _F\leqslant O(\frac{k^2}{\alpha}\delta^{1 / 4}\Delta^{1 / 2}).$

Now, let  $V_{\tau} \subseteq V'$  be a  $\tau = \delta^{1/4}$ -cover, in  $\ell_2$ -norm, of vectors with  $\ell_2$  norm at most  $2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$  in  $V'$ . Then, since  $\left\| \mu_i \right\|_2 \leqslant \frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}$ , there is a vector  $\hat{\mu}_i \in V_\tau$  such that  $\left\| \mu_i - \hat{\mu}_i \right\|_2^2 \leqslant \tau + \frac{20}{\alpha^2} \sqrt{\delta} \Delta \leqslant \frac{40}{\alpha^2} \sqrt{\delta} \Delta$ .

Further, there exist  $\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots \tau_{O(k^{2})}$  in a  $\tau$  -cover of  $[-10(1 + \Delta^2) / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^5),10(1 + \Delta^2) / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^5)]$  and vectors  $v_{1},v_{2},\dots ,v_{O(k^{2})}\in V_{\tau}$  such that  $\left\| \sum_{i = 1}^{O(k^2)}\tau_iv_i v_i^\top -Q_i\right\| _F\leqslant O(k^4\delta^{1 / 4}\Delta^{1 / 2} / \alpha)$ . In particular,  $\hat{\Sigma}_i = I + \hat{S} -\sum_{i = 1}^{O(k^2)}\tau_iv_iv_i^\top$  satisfies

$$
\left\| \hat {\Sigma} _ {i} - \Sigma_ {i} \right\| _ {F} = O (\sqrt {\eta}) + O \left(\frac {k ^ {4} \delta^ {1 / 4} \Delta^ {1 / 2}}{\alpha}\right) = O \left(\frac {k ^ {4} \delta^ {1 / 4} \Delta^ {1 / 2}}{\alpha}\right). \tag {3.1}
$$

The size of this search space for every fixed  $\hat{S} \in L'$  can be bounded above by  $\left(\frac{1 + \Delta^2}{\delta \alpha^5}\right)^{\mathcal{O}(k^5 \dim(V'))}$ . Thus, the size of  $L$  can be bounded from above by

$$
k ^ {5} \left(\frac {\Delta^ {4}}{\eta} + \frac {1}{\alpha^ {4} \eta}\right) ^ {4 k} \cdot \left(\frac {1 + \Delta^ {2}}{\delta \sqrt {\eta} \alpha^ {5}}\right) ^ {O (k ^ {5} \dim (V ^ {\prime}))} \leqslant \exp \left(\log (1 / \varepsilon) (k + 1 / \alpha + \Delta) ^ {O (k)} / \eta^ {2}\right).
$$

This completes the proof.

# 3.3 Robust Estimation of Hermite Tensors

In this section, we will prove Proposition 3.3.

Proof of Proposition 3.3. Consider the uniform distribution on the uncorrupted sample  $X$ . We want to analyze the effect of applying the robust mean estimation algorithm (Fact 2.36) to the points  $h_m(x)$ , for  $x \in X$ . In order for us to apply Fact 2.36, we need to ensure that the uniform distribution

on  $\{h_m(x)\}_{x\in X}$  has bounded covariance. This step gives us a good approximation to  $\mathbb{E}_{x\sim_uX}h_m(x)$ . In order for us to obtain an approximation to  $\mathbb{E}h_m(\mathcal{M})$ , we need to bound the difference between  $\mathbb{E}h_m(\mathcal{M})$  and  $\mathbb{E}_{x\sim_uX}h_m(x)$ . We will do both these steps below.

The second part is immediate. By the definition of  $h_m(X)$ , we have that

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{| X |} \sum_ {x \in X} h _ {m} (x) - \mathbb {E} h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \sum_ {j \leqslant m / 2} m ^ {2 j} d ^ {j} \left\| \frac {1}{| X |} \sum_ {x \in X} x ^ {\otimes (m - 2 j)} - \mathbb {E} \mathcal {M} ^ {\otimes (m - 2 j)} \right\| _ {F}.
$$

By Lemma 2.49, this is at most  $O(1 + \Delta + 1 / \alpha)^m m^{O(m)} d^{m/2} \gamma \leqslant \eta/2$ . We note that a similar argument bounds

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{| X |} \sum_ {x \in X} h _ {m} (x) \otimes h _ {m} (x) - \mathbb {E} h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) \otimes h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \eta^ {2}.
$$

Let us now verify the first part. We proceed via bounding the operator norm of the covariance of  $h_m(\mathcal{M})$ . We can then use the bound on the Frobenius norm  $\left\| \frac{1}{|X|} \sum_{x \in X} h_m(x) \otimes h_m(X) - \mathbb{E} h_m(\mathcal{M}) \otimes h_m(\mathcal{M}) \right\|_F$  to get a bound on  $\left\| \frac{1}{|X|} \sum_{x \in X} h_m(x) h_m(x)^{\top} \right\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  (the operator norm of the canonical square flattening of the of the  $2m$ -th empirical Hermite moment tensor of  $X$ ). This will complete the proof.

Let  $G_{i} = \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$  be the components of  $\mathcal{M}$ . We have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {C o v} \left(h _ {m} (\mathcal {M})\right) = \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \operatorname {C o v} \left(h _ {m} \left(G _ {i}\right)\right) \\ + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i, j \in [ k ]} w _ {i} w _ {j} \left(\mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {m} \left(G _ {i}\right) \right] - \mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {m} \left(G _ {j}\right) \right]\right) \left(\mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {m} \left(G _ {i}\right) \right] - \mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {m} \left(G _ {j}\right) \right]\right) ^ {\top}. \tag {3.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

By Lemma 2.8, we have that for all  $i \in [k]$ , it holds

$$
\| \mathsf {C o v} (h _ {m} (G _ {i})) \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \mathcal {O} \left(m (1 + \left\| \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {2} + \| \Sigma_ {i} - I \| _ {F})\right) ^ {2 m} = \mathcal {O} \left(m (1 + 2 / \sqrt {\alpha} + \Delta)\right) ^ {2 m},
$$

where for any matrix  $M$ ,  $\| M \|_{\mathrm{op}} = \max_{\| u \|_2 = 1} \| Mu \|_2$  is the operator norm of the matrix. Further, for any  $i, j \in [k]$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (G _ {i}) ] - \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (G _ {j}) ]\right) \left(\mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (G _ {i}) ] - \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (G _ {j}) ]\right) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \left\| \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (G _ {i}) ] - \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (G _ {j}) ] \right\| _ {2} \tag {3.3} \\ = \mathcal {O} \left(m (1 + 1 / \alpha + \Delta)\right) ^ {2 m}. \\ \end{array}
$$

This claim follows from the triangle inequality of the operator norm.

# 3.4 List-Recovery of Covariances up to Low-Rank Error

In this section, we prove Proposition 3.4. We first set some useful notation. We will write  $S_{i} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \Sigma_{i} - I$  throughout this section. We will also use  $S_{i}'$  to denote  $S_{i} + \mu_{i} \otimes \mu_{i}$ .

We first show that for every  $i$ , there exists a matrix  $P$  such that  $\left(\sum_{i\in [k]}w_iS_i'\otimes S_i'\right)(\cdot ,\cdot ,P)$  is close to  $S_{i}^{\prime}$ .

Lemma 3.6 (Existence of a 2-Tensor). Under the hypothesis of Proposition 3.4, for each  $i \in [k]$ , there exists a matrix  $P$  such that  $\| P \|_F = O\big(1 / (\sqrt{\eta} \alpha)\big)$  and  $\left\| T_4'(\cdot, \cdot, P) - S_i' \right\|_F = O\bigg(\sqrt{\eta / \alpha}\bigg)$ , where  $T_4' = \left(\sum_{i \in [k]} w_i S_i' \otimes S_i'\right)$ .

Note that throughout this section it will be useful to think of  $T_4'$  as a  $d^2 \times d^2$  matrix rather than as a tensor. In this case, we can think of  $T_4'$  as  $\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i(S_i') (S_i')^T$ . From standard facts about positive semidefinite matrices it follows that  $S_i'$  is in the image of  $T_4'$ , and Lemma 3.6 is just a slightly robustified version of this (saying that we can find an approximate preimage that it not itself too large).

The proof of this Lemma 3.6 will involve linear programming duality with an infinite system of constraints. As the application of duality with infinitely many constraints has some technical issues, we state below an appropriate version of duality.

Fact 3.7 (Linear Programming Duality for Compact, Convex Constraint Sets). Let  $K \subset \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$  be a compact convex set. There exists an  $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$  so that  $(x, 1) \cdot z > 0$  for all  $z \in K$  if and only if there is no element  $(0, 0, \ldots, 0, a) \in K$  for any  $a \leqslant 0$ .

This fact can be proved by noting that if no such  $a$  exists, there must be a hyperplane separating  $K$  from the set of such points  $(0, a)$ . This separating hyperplane will be of the form  $(z, y) \in H$  if and only if  $y = x \cdot z$  for some  $x$  and this  $x$  will provide the solution to the linear system.

Proof of Lemma 3.6. To show that such a  $P$  exists for each  $i$ , we apply linear programming duality. In particular, the conditions imposed on  $P$  define a linear program, which has a feasible solution unless there is a solution to the dual linear program. For sufficiently large constants  $c_{1}$  and  $c_{2}$ , consider the following primal in the variable  $P$ :

$$
\langle v, P \rangle \leqslant \frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha} \| v \| _ {F} \quad \forall v \in \mathbb {R} ^ {d \times d} \tag {3.4}
$$

$$
\left\langle u, T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (\cdot , \cdot , P) - S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle \leqslant c _ {2} \sqrt {\eta} \| u \| _ {F} \quad \forall u \in \mathbb {R} ^ {d \times d}. \tag {3.5}
$$

It is not hard to see that  $\| P\| _F\leqslant \frac{c_1}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}$  if and only if (3.4) holds for all  $v$  and  $\left\| T_4'(\cdot ,\cdot ,P) - S_i'\right\| _F\leqslant c_2\sqrt{\eta / \alpha}$  if and only if (3.5) holds for all  $u$ . Throughout the proof, we suggest that the reader think of  $u$  and  $v$  as vectors in  $d^2$ -dimensional vector space.

Our goal is to show that there exists a feasible solution  $P$  such that (3.4) and (3.5) hold simultaneously for all  $u, v \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ . We first note that this is equivalent to saying that

$$
\langle v, P \rangle + \left\langle u, T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (\cdot , \cdot , P) \right\rangle - \left\langle u, S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle \leqslant \frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha} \| v \| _ {F} + c _ {2} \sqrt {\eta} \| u \| _ {F}, \tag {3.6}
$$

for all  $u, v \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ . This is not quite in the form necessary to apply Fact 3.7, so we note that this is in turn equivalent to saying that

$$
\left\langle v, P \right\rangle + \left\langle u, T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (\cdot , \cdot , P) \right\rangle - \left\langle u, S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle \leqslant 1, \tag {3.7}
$$

for all  $u, v \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$  so that  $\frac{c_1}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha} \|v\|_F + c_2\sqrt{\eta} \|u\|_F \leqslant 1$ , and  $u \in \operatorname{span}\{S_i'\}$ . As this is a convex set of linear equations, we have by Fact 3.7 that there exists such a  $P$  unless there exists such a pair of

$u$  and  $v$  so that the coefficient of  $P$  in Equation (3.7) is 0 and so that the resulting inequality of constants is either false or holds with equality. In particular, the coefficient of  $P$  vanishes if and only if  $v = -T_4'(u,\cdot,\cdot)$ . We then get a contradiction only if for some  $u \in \operatorname{span}\{S_i'\}$

$$
- \left\langle u, S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle \geqslant 1 \geqslant \frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha} \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u, \cdot , \cdot) \right\| _ {F} + c _ {2} \sqrt {\eta} \| u \| _ {F}. \tag {3.8}
$$

We claim that this is impossible.

In particular, squaring Equation (3.8) would give

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle u, S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle^ {2} \geqslant \left(\frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha} \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u, \cdot , \cdot) \right\| _ {F} + c _ {2} \sqrt {\eta} \| u \| _ {F}\right) ^ {2} \tag {3.9} \\ \geqslant \frac {c}{\alpha} \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u, \cdot , \cdot) \right\| _ {F} \cdot \| u \| _ {F}, \\ \end{array}
$$

for some large enough constant  $c > 1$ , where the last inequality follows from the AM-GM inequality. However, using the dual characterization of the Frobenius norm, we have

$$
\left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u, \cdot , \cdot) \right\| _ {F} \geqslant \frac {\left\langle u , T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u , \cdot , \cdot) \right\rangle}{\| u \| _ {F}} \geqslant \frac {w _ {i}}{\| u \| _ {F}} \left\langle u, S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle^ {2}, \tag {3.10}
$$

where the last inequality follows from  $T_4'$  containing a  $w_i S_i \otimes S_i$  term, and the other terms contributing non-negatively. Rearranging Equation (3.10), we have

$$
\left\langle u, S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\rangle^ {2} \leqslant \frac {1}{w _ {i}} \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u, \cdot , \cdot) \right\| _ {F} \| u \| _ {F} \leqslant \frac {1}{\alpha} \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u, \cdot , \cdot) \right\| _ {F} \| u \| _ {F}.
$$

This contradicts Equation (3.9) unless  $T_4'(u, \cdot, \cdot) = 0$ . This therefore suffices to prove the feasibility of the primal.

We have thus shown that there is some matrix  $P$  so that  $T_4'(P, \cdot, \cdot)$  suffices for our purposes. We need to show that our appropriate random linear combination of  $x^{(j)} \otimes y^{(j)}$  suffices. In fact, we will show that with reasonably high probability over our choice of  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)}$  that there is some linear combination of the  $x^{(j)} \otimes y^{(j)}$  (with coefficients that are not too large) so that their projection onto the space spanned by the  $S_j'$  (which is all that matters when applying  $T_4'$ ) equal to  $P$ .

For the sake of intuition, we note that if we removed the bound on the coefficients, we would need that the projections of the  $x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}$  spanned  $\operatorname {span}\{S_i'\}$ . Since there are at least  $k$  of them, this will hold unless there is some  $v\in \operatorname {span}\{S_i'\}$  so that  $v$  is orthogonal to all of the  $x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}$ . This shouldn't happen because each  $x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}$  is very unlikely to be orthogonal to  $v$ .

To deal with the constraint that the coefficients are not too large, we use linear programming duality to show that there will be a solution unless there is some  $v$  that is nearly orthogonal to all of the  $x^{(j)} \otimes y^{(j)}$ . Again, this is unlikely to happen for any individual term, and thus, by independence, highly unlikely to happen for all  $j$  simultaneously. Combining this with a cover argument will give our proof.

Lemma 3.8 (Existence of a Bi-Linear Form). Given the preconditions in Proposition 3.4, with probability at least  $99/100$  over the choice of  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)}$ , there exist  $b_{j} \in [-D, D]$  for  $j \in [4k]$ , where  $D = O\big(k^4 / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha)\big)$ , such that the projection of  $\sum_{j=1}^{t} b_{j} x^{(j)} \otimes y^{(j)}$  onto the space spanned by the  $S_{i}'$  is  $P$ , where  $P$  satisfies the conclusion of Proposition 3.6.

Proof. To prove this lemma, we again use a linear programming based argument. Consider the following (primal) linear program in the variables  $b_{j}$ , for  $j \in [4k]$ :

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} b _ {j} \left\langle S _ {i} ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle = \left\langle S _ {i} ^ {\prime}, P \right\rangle \quad \forall i \in [ k ] \tag {3.11}
$$

$$
- D \leqslant b _ {j} \leqslant D \quad \forall j \in [ 4 k ] \tag {3.12}
$$

We note that a set of  $b_{j}$  satisfying Equation (3.11) will have the projection of  $\sum_{j\in [4k]}b_jx^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}$  onto the span of the  $S_i^\prime$  be the same as the projection of  $P$ , and that if the  $b_{j}$ 's satisfy Equation (3.12) then we will have  $|b_{j}| \leqslant D$  for all  $j$ . Thus, it suffices to show that with high probability over our choice of  $x^{(j)}$  and  $y^{(j)}$  that the above system is feasible.

We will show this by linear programming duality (since this is now a finite system of equations, we can use standard results rather than Fact 3.7). In particular, we have that Equations (3.11) and (3.12) are simultaneously satisfiable unless there are real numbers  $c_{i}$  and non-negative real numbers  $z_{j}, z_{j}'$  so that

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} c _ {i} \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} b _ {j} \langle S _ {i} ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} (z _ {j} - z _ {j} ^ {\prime}) b _ {j} \leqslant \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} c _ {i} \langle S _ {i} ^ {\prime}, P \rangle + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} (z _ {j} + z _ {j} ^ {\prime}) D
$$

yields a contradiction. Setting  $v = \sum_{i=1}^{k} c_i S_i'$ , the above simplifies to

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} b _ {j} \left(\langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle + z _ {j} - z _ {j} ^ {\prime}\right) \leqslant \langle v, P \rangle + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left(z _ {j} + z _ {j} ^ {\prime}\right) D \tag {3.13}
$$

We note that in order for Equation (3.13) to be a contradiction, it must be the case that the coefficients of  $b_{j}$  are all 0. In particular, we must have

$$
z _ {j} ^ {\prime} - z _ {j} = \langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle
$$

for all  $j$ . In particular, this means that

$$
z _ {j} + z _ {j} ^ {\prime} \geqslant \left| \langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle \right|.
$$

In such a case, the right hand side of Equation (3.13) will be at least

$$
\langle v, P \rangle + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle \right| D
$$

Therefore, Equation (3.13) can only yield a contradiction if there exists a  $v \in \operatorname{span}\{S_i'\}$  so that

$$
\langle v, P \rangle <   - \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle \right| D. \tag {3.14}
$$

We want to show that with high probability over our choice of  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)}$  that there is no  $v \in \operatorname{span}\{S_i'\}$  satisfying Equation (3.14). In fact, we will show that for every such  $v$  that

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \rangle \right| \geqslant \frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha} \| v \| _ {F}.
$$

We can scale  $v$  so that  $\| v \|_F = 1$ , and it suffices to show that

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \left\langle \tilde {v}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \right| \geqslant \left(\frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D}\right) \tag {3.15}
$$

holds for all unit vectors  $v$  in  $\text{span}\{S_j'\}$  with high probability.

Since we need to show that infinitely many equations all hold with high probability, we will use a cover argument. In particular, we can construct  $C$ , a  $\tau$ -cover for all unit vectors  $v$  in the span of the  $S_{i'}'$ , where we take  $\tau = \left(\frac{c_1'}{k^2\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)$ . Since this is a cover of a unit sphere in a  $k$ -dimensional subspace, we can construct such a cover so that  $|C| = O(1 / \tau)^k$ . Replacing  $v$  with the closest point in  $C$ , denoted by  $v'$ , it suffices to show that with high probability for all  $v$  that

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} | \left\langle v, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle | \geqslant \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} | \left\langle v ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle | - \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} | \left\langle v - v ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle | \geqslant \left(\frac {2 c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D}\right). \tag {3.16}
$$

We begin by bounding the terms

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \left\langle v - v ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \right|.
$$

For this we notice by Cauchy-Schwartz that each term is at most  $\| v - v' \|_F$  times the Frobenius norm of the projection of  $x^{(j)} \otimes y^{(j)}$  onto the span of the  $S_i'$ . We note that for any  $k$ -dimensional subspace  $W$  with orthonormal basis  $w_1, \ldots, w_k$  we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| \mathrm {P r o j} _ {W} (x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)}) \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \left| \left\langle w _ {i}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \right| ^ {2} \\ = k. \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, with high probability over the choice of  $x^{(j)}$ ,  $y^{(j)}$  each of the projections of  $x^{(j)} \otimes y^{(j)}$  onto the span of the  $S_{i}^{\prime}$  has Frobenius norm  $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{k})$ . Therefore, if this condition holds over our choice of  $x^{(j)}$  and  $y^{(j)}$ , we can show Equation (3.16) if we can show that

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \left\langle v ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \right| \geqslant \left(\frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D}\right) \geqslant \left(\frac {2 c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D}\right) - \tau \tilde {O} \left(k ^ {3 / 2}\right) \tag {3.17}
$$

for all  $v^{\prime}\in C$

Each term in  $\sum_{j\in [4k]}\left\langle v',x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle$  is a random bi-linear form given by  $z_{j} = \sum_{\ell ,p\in [d]}v_{\ell ,p}^{\prime}x_{\ell}^{(j)}y_{p}^{(j)}$ . Then, we have that  $\mathbb{E}\left[z_j\right] = 0$  and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ z _ {j} ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(\sum_ {\ell , p \in [ d ]} v _ {\ell , p} ^ {\prime} x _ {\ell} ^ {(j)} y _ {p} ^ {(j)}\right) ^ {2} \right] = \sum_ {\ell , \ell^ {\prime}, p, p ^ {\prime}} \mathbb {E} \left[ v _ {\ell , p} ^ {\prime} v _ {\ell^ {\prime}, p ^ {\prime}} ^ {\prime} x _ {\ell} ^ {(j)} x _ {\ell^ {\prime}} ^ {(j)} y _ {p} ^ {(j)} y _ {p ^ {\prime}} ^ {(j)} \right] \\ = \sum_ {\ell , p \in [ d ]} \left(v _ {\ell , p} ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \cdot \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(x _ {\ell} ^ {(j)}\right) ^ {2} \right] \cdot \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(y _ {p} ^ {(j)}\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = 1, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last equality follows from  $\tilde{v}_F' = 1$ .

Using Lemma 2.10 with  $\zeta = \frac{2c_1}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {P} \left[ \left| z _ {j} \right| \leqslant \frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D} \right] \leqslant c _ {5} \left(\frac {2 c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D}\right) ^ {1 / 2}. \tag {3.18}
$$

However, we note that Equation (3.17) will hold unless  $\left|z_{j}\right| \leqslant \frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta} \alpha D}$  for all  $j \in [4k]$ . Since the  $z_{j}$ 's are independent, we conclude that

$$
\mathbb {P} \left[ \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} \left| \left\langle v ^ {\prime}, x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \right| \leqslant \frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D} \right] \leqslant O \left(\frac {c _ {1}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha D}\right) ^ {2 k}. \tag {3.19}
$$

Since the above argument holds for any  $v' \in C$ , we can union bound over all elements in the cover  $C$ , and the probability that there exists a  $\tilde{v}'$  in the cover that does not satisfy Equation (3.17) is at most  $O\left(k^2 \sqrt{\eta} \alpha D\right)^k \cdot O\left(\frac{c_1}{\sqrt{\eta} \alpha D}\right)^{2k}$ . Setting  $D$  to be a sufficiently large multiple of  $(k^4 / (\sqrt{\eta} \alpha))$  suffices to conclude that with probability at least  $1 - 1/poly(k)$ , the primal is feasible.

Proof of Proposition 3.4. We begin by bounding the Frobenius norm of  $\hat{T}_4$ . Let  $T_4 = \mathbb{E}[h_4(X)]$ . It then follows from Lemma 2.6 that

$$
T _ {4} = \operatorname {S y m} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} w _ {i} \left(3 S _ {i} \otimes S _ {i} + 6 S _ {i} \otimes \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 2} + \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 4}\right)\right). \tag {3.20}
$$

Further,  $\| S_i\otimes S_i\| _F\leqslant \| S_i\| _F^2\leqslant \Delta^2$ $\left\| S_{i}\otimes \mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}\right\|_{F}\leqslant \left\| S_{i}\right\|_{F}\left\| \mu_{i}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant 4\Delta /\alpha ,$  and  $\left\| \mu_i^{\otimes 4}\right\| _F\leqslant \left\| \mu_i\right\| _2^4\leqslant$ $16 / \alpha^2$  . Since  $T_{4}$  is an average of terms of the form  $S_{i}^{\otimes 2},S_{i}\otimes \mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}$  and  $\mu_i^{\otimes 4}$  , and each such term is upper bounded, we can conclude that  $\| T_4\| _F = O\big(\Delta^2 +1 / \alpha^2\big)$  , and by the triangle inequality that  $\left\| \hat{T}_4\right\| _F\leqslant O\big(\Delta^2 +1 / \alpha^2 +\eta \big)$  . Let  $S_{i}^{\prime} = S_{i} + \mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}$  and let  $T_4'\coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^k w_i(S_i'\otimes S_i')$  . We can then rewrite Equation (3.20) as follows:

$$
T _ {4} = \mathrm {S y m} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} w _ {i} \left(3 S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \otimes S _ {i} ^ {\prime} - 2 \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 4}\right)\right). \tag {3.21}
$$

For  $j \in [4k]$ , let  $x^{(j)}, y^{(j)} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I)$ . Collapsing two modes of  $\hat{T}_4$ , it follows from Equation (3.21) that for any fixed  $j$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {T} _ {4} \left(\cdot , \cdot , x ^ {(j)}, y ^ {(j)}\right) = \left(\hat {T} _ {4} - T _ {4}\right) \left(\cdot , \cdot , x ^ {(j)}, y ^ {(j)}\right) + T _ {4} \left(\cdot , \cdot , x ^ {(j)}, y ^ {(j)}\right) \\ = \left(\hat {T} _ {4} - T _ {4}\right) \left(\dots , \dots , x ^ {(j)}, y ^ {(j)}\right) + \operatorname {S y m} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} w _ {i} \left(3 S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \otimes S _ {i} ^ {\prime} - 2 \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 4}\right)\right) \left(\dots , \dots , x ^ {(j)}, y ^ {(j)}\right) (3.22) \\ = \left(\hat {T} _ {4} - T _ {4} + T _ {4} ^ {\prime}\right) \left(\dots , \dots , x ^ {(j)}, y ^ {(j)}\right) + \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} x ^ {(j)}\right) \otimes \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} y ^ {(j)}\right) (3.22) \\ + \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} y ^ {(j)}\right) \otimes \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} x ^ {(j)}\right) + \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \left(- 2 \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 2} \left<   \mu_ {i}, x ^ {(j)} \right> \left<   \mu_ {i}, y ^ {(j)} \right > \right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use that  $\operatorname{Sym}(\cdot)$  is a linear operator satisfying  $\operatorname{Sym}\left(\mu_i^{\otimes 4}\right) = \mu_i^{\otimes 4}$ , and

$$
\mathrm {S y m} \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \otimes S _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) = \frac {1}{3} S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \otimes S _ {i} ^ {\prime} + \frac {1}{3} S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \oplus S _ {i} ^ {\prime} + \frac {1}{3} S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \ominus S _ {i} ^ {\prime}
$$

where for indices  $(i_1, i_2, i_3, i_4), (S_i' \oplus S_i') (i_1, i_2, i_3, i_4) = (S_i' \otimes S_i') (i_1, i_3, i_2, i_4)$  and  $(S_i' \ominus S_i') (i_1, i_2, i_3, i_4) = (S_i' \otimes S_i') (i_1, i_4, i_2, i_3)$ .

Next, it follows from Lemma 3.6 that there exists a matrix  $\tilde{P}_i$  such that  $\left\| \tilde{P}_i\right\|_F = O\big(1 / (\sqrt{\eta}\alpha)\big)$  and  $\left\| T_4'(\cdot ,\cdot ,\tilde{P}_i) - S_i'\right\| _F = O\Big(\sqrt{\eta / \alpha}\Big)$ . Furthermore, with probability at least 0.99, there exists a sequence of  $b_{j}\in [-D,D]$ , for  $j\in [4k]$ , such that  $T_4^\prime (\cdot ,\cdot ,\sum_{j\in [4k]}b_jx^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}) = T_4^\prime (\cdot ,\cdot ,\tilde{P}_i)$ .

Consider a cover,  $C$ , of the interval  $[-D, D]$  with points spaced at intervals of length  $\tau = O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\eta}}{\alpha k\left(\Delta^4 + 1 / \alpha^4\right)}\right)$ . Since we uniformly sample  $a_j$ 's, with probability at least  $(\tau / D)^{O(k)}$ , for all  $j \in [4k]$ ,  $|b_j - a_j| \leqslant \tau$ , and we condition on this event. Thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} \left(\cdot , \cdot , \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} x ^ {(j)} y ^ {(j)}\right) - S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} \left(\cdot , \cdot , \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} b _ {j} x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)}\right) - S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {F} + \left\| T _ {4} ^ {\prime} \left(\cdot , \cdot , \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} (b _ {j} - a _ {j}) x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)}\right) \right\| _ {F} \\ \leqslant O \left(\sqrt {\eta / \alpha}\right) + O \left(\tau \Delta^ {2}\right) \leqslant O \left(\sqrt {\eta / \alpha}\right). \tag {3.23} \\ \end{array}
$$

Taking the linear combinations with coefficients  $a_{j}$  in Equation (3.22), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {T} _ {4} \left(\cdot , \cdot , \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)}\right) - S _ {i} = \left(\hat {T} _ {4} - T _ {4} + T _ {4} ^ {\prime}\right) \left(\cdot , \cdot , \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} x ^ {(j)} \otimes y ^ {(j)}\right) - S _ {i} ^ {\prime} - \mu_ {i} \otimes \mu_ {i} \\ + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} x ^ {(j)}\right) \otimes \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} y ^ {(j)}\right) + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} y ^ {(j)}\right) \otimes \left(S _ {i} ^ {\prime} x ^ {(j)}\right) \\ + \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \left(- 2 \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 2} \left\langle \mu_ {i}, x ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \left\langle \mu_ {i}, y ^ {(j)} \right\rangle\right). \tag {3.24} \\ \end{array}
$$

Setting  $P_{i} = \left(\hat{T}_{4} - T_{4} + T_{4}^{\prime}\right)\left(\cdot ,\cdot ,\sum_{j\in [4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right) - S_{i}^{\prime}$ , it follows from Lemma 2.9 that with probability at least 0.99,  $\left(\hat{T}_4 - T_4\right)\left(\cdot ,\cdot ,\sum_{j\in [4k]}a_jx^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right)$  has Frobenius norm  $O(kD\eta)$  and it follows from Equation (3.23) that with probability at least 0.99,  $T_4^\prime \left(\cdot ,\cdot ,\sum_{j\in [4k]}a_jx^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right) - S_i'$  has Frobenius norm  $O\Big(\sqrt{\eta / \alpha}\Big)$ . Setting the remaining terms to  $Q_{i}$ , with probability at least 0.99 we can bound their

Frobenius norm as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| Q _ {i} \| _ {F} \leqslant \left\| \mu_ {i} \otimes \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {F} + \left\| \left(\sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \oplus S _ {i} ^ {\prime} + w _ {i} S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \ominus S _ {i} ^ {\prime} - 2 w _ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 4}\right) \left(\cdot , \cdot , \sum_ {j \in [ 4 k ]} a _ {j} x ^ {(j)} y ^ {(j)}\right) \right\| _ {F} \\ \leqslant \frac {4}{\alpha} + \left(2 \max  _ {i \in k} \| S _ {i} ^ {\prime} \| _ {F} ^ {2} + \frac {3 2}{\alpha^ {2}} + k \tau\right) \cdot \| \tilde {P} \| _ {F} \tag {3.25} \\ \leqslant \frac {4}{\alpha} + O \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha} \left(\Delta + \frac {1}{\alpha}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \leqslant O \left(\frac {1 + \Delta^ {2}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha^ {3}}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first inequality follows from the triangle inequality, the second follows from our assumptions that  $\left\| \mu_i\right\| _2\leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha},\sum_{j\in [4k]}b_jx^{(j)}y^{(j)} = \tilde{P}_i$  in the span of the  $S_{i}^{\prime}$ , and  $\left|a_j - b_j\right|\leqslant \tau$  for all  $j\in [4k]$ , and the third inequality follows from the definition of  $S_{i}^{\prime}$ , the bound on  $\left\| \tilde{P}\right\| _F$  and the bound on  $\| S_i - I\| _F$ .

# 3.5 Finding a Low-dimensional Subspace for Exhaustive Search

In this subsection, we will prove Proposition 3.5.

We start by extending Theorem 4 of [MV10], which shows that large parameter distance between pairs of univariate Gaussian mixtures implies large distance between their low-degree moments. In the following, we use  $M_{j}(F) = \mathbb{E}\left[F^{j}\right]$  to denote the  $j$ -th moment of a distribution  $F$ . We show:

Lemma 3.9. There exists a constant  $C > 0$  such that the following holds: Fix any  $D > 0$  and  $0 \leqslant \beta \leqslant 1 / (2(2k - 1)!D^{2k - 3})$ . Suppose that  $F = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \sigma_i^2)$  is a univariate  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians with  $w_i \geqslant \beta$ , and  $|\mu_i|, \sigma_i \leqslant D$ , for all  $i \in [k]$ . If  $|\mu_i| + |\sigma_i^2 - 1| \geqslant \beta$  for some  $i \leqslant k$ , then

$$
\max _ {j \in [ 2 k ]} \left| M _ {j} (F) - M _ {j} \left(\mathcal {N} (0, 1)\right) \right| \geqslant \beta^ {C ^ {k + 1} (k + 1)! - 1}.
$$

We give the proof of Lemma 3.9 in Section 3.6.

Lemma 3.10 (Bounding  $\mu_{i}$  's and  $S_{i}$  's in non-influential directions for  $\mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})]$ ). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  satisfying  $w_i \geqslant \alpha$ ,  $\left\| \mu_i \right\|_2 \leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}$ , and  $\|\Sigma_i - I\|_F \leqslant \Delta$  for every  $i \in [k]$ . For some  $B \in \mathbb{R}$ , let  $u \in \mathbb{R}^d$  be a unit vector such that  $|\mathbb{E}[h_m(\langle \mathcal{M}, u \rangle)]| \leqslant B$  for all  $m \in [2k]$ . Then, for  $\delta = 2^{O(k)} B^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$  and  $S_i = \Sigma_i - I$ , we have that:

1. for all  $i \leqslant k$ ,  $\left|\langle u, \mu_i \rangle \right|$ ,  $|u^\top (I - \Sigma_i)u| \leqslant \delta$  
2.  $\| S_i u\| _2^2\leqslant 20\delta \Delta /\alpha^2 +B / \alpha ,$

where  $C > 0$  is a fixed universal constant.

Proof. The 1-D random variable  $\langle u, \mathcal{M} \rangle$  is a mixture of Gaussians described by  $\sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\langle \mu_i, u \rangle, u^\top \Sigma_i u)$ . Towards a contradiction, assume that there is an  $i \in [k]$  such that

$\left|\langle u,\mu_i\rangle \right| + |u^{\top}(I - \Sigma_i)u|\geqslant \delta$  . Then, applying Lemma 3.9, yields that there is a  $j\in [2k]$  such that  $|M_{j}(\langle u,\mathcal{M}\rangle) - M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))|\geqslant \delta^{C^{k + 1}(k + 1)! - 1}$  . Applying Fact 2.5 implies that there exists an  $m\in [2k]$  such that

$$
\left| \mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {m} (\langle u, \mathcal {M} \rangle) \right] \right| > 2 ^ {- O (k)} \delta^ {C ^ {k + 1} (k + 1)! - 1} \gg B ,
$$

yielding a contradiction.

We can now prove the second part. Recall that for  $S_{i} = \Sigma_{i} - I$  for every  $i$ , we have that

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {4} (\mathcal {M}) \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} w _ {i} \mathrm {S y m} \left(3 \left(S _ {i} \otimes S _ {i}\right) + 6 \left(S _ {i} \otimes \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 2}\right) + \mu_ {i} ^ {\otimes 4}\right).
$$

We consider the  $d \times d$  matrix obtained by the natural flattening of the  $d \times d$  tensor  $u^{\otimes 2} \cdot \mathbb{E}$ $[h_4(\mathcal{M})]$ . Then, we can write:

$$
\begin{array}{l} u ^ {\otimes 2} \cdot \mathbb {E} \left[ h _ {4} (\mathcal {M}) \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} w _ {i} \Big ((u ^ {\top} S _ {i} u) S _ {i} + 2 (S _ {i} u) (S _ {i} u) ^ {\top} + \langle u, \mu_ {i} \rangle^ {2} S _ {i} \\ + 2 \langle u, \mu_ {i} \rangle \mu_ {i} (S _ {i} u) ^ {\top} + 2 \langle u, \mu_ {i} \rangle (S _ {i} u) \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} + (u ^ {\top} S _ {i} u) \mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} + \langle u, \mu_ {i} \rangle^ {2} \mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top}) . \tag {3.26} \\ \end{array}
$$

Now, from the first part, we know that for all  $i \in [k]$ ,  $|u^{\top}S_{i}u| \leqslant \delta$  and the hypothesis of the lemma gives us that  $\| S_{i}\|_{F} = \| \Sigma_{i} - I\|_{F} \leqslant \Delta$ . Thus, for each  $i$ , the first term in the summation above has Frobenius norm at most  $\Delta \delta$ . Using that  $\langle u,\mu_i\rangle_2^2 \leqslant \delta^2$  from the first part of the lemma, yields that, for each  $i$ , the Frobenius norm of the third term is at most  $\Delta \delta^{2}$ .

Next, using in addition that  $\left\| \mu_i\right\| _2\leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$  yields that, for each  $i$ , the Frobenius norm of the 4th and 5th terms are at most  $2\delta \Delta /\sqrt{\alpha}$  and the Frobenius norm of the 6th and 7th terms are at most  $\delta /\alpha$ . Thus, for each  $i$  and all but the 2nd term in the summation above, we have an upper bound on the Frobenius norm of  $4\delta \Delta /\alpha$ .

Now, since  $|\mathbb{E}[h_4(\langle \mathcal{M}, u \rangle)]| \leqslant B$ , and  $u$  is a unit vector, we have that  $\left\| u^{\otimes 2} \mathbb{E}[h_4(\mathcal{M})] \right\|_F \leqslant B$ . Thus, combining the aforementioned argument with the triangle inequality, we have for each  $i$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| S _ {i} u \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| S _ {i} u (S _ {i} u) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \frac {1}{\alpha} \left\| u ^ {\otimes 2} \cdot \mathbb {E} [ h _ {4} (\mathcal {M}) ] \right\| _ {F} + \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \Big (\Big (u ^ {\top} S _ {i} u + \Big <   u, \mu_ {i} \Big > ^ {2} \Big) \| S _ {i} \| _ {F} \Big) \\ + \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} 4 w _ {i} \Big (\big (\big \langle u, \mu_ {i} \big \rangle \big) \big \| \mu_ {i} \big \| _ {2} \| S _ {i} u \| _ {2} \Big) + \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} 4 w _ {i} \Big (\big (\big \langle u, \mu_ {i} \big \rangle^ {2} + u ^ {\top} S _ {i} u \big) \big \| \mu_ {i} \big \| _ {2} ^ {2} \Big) \\ \leqslant B / \alpha + 1 5 \delta \Delta / \alpha , \\ \end{array}
$$

and the claim follows.

Lemma 3.11 (Subspace covering all the means and large singular vectors of  $S_{i} = \Sigma_{i} - I$ ). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  satisfying  $w_{i} \geqslant \alpha$ ,  $\left\| \mu_{i} \right\|_{2} \leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$ , and  $\| \Sigma_{i} - I \|_F \leqslant \Delta$  for all  $i \in [k]$ . Given  $0 < \eta < 1$ , let  $\hat{T}_m$  satisfy  $\left\| \hat{T}_m - \mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})] \right\|_F \leqslant \eta$  for every  $m \in [4k]$  and let  $\lambda \geqslant 2\eta$ . Let  $V$  be the span of all the left singular vectors of the  $d \times d^{m-1}$  matrix obtained by the natural flattening of  $\hat{T}_m$  with singular values at least  $\lambda$ . Then, for  $\delta = 2\lambda^{1/(2C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$ , we have that:

1.  $\dim (V)\leqslant \left(4k\eta^2 +k^{O(k)}\right)O(1 + 1 / \alpha +\Delta)^{4k} / \lambda^2,$  
2. Let

$V_{\mathrm{inf}} = \{\mu_i\}_{i\in [k]}\cup \left\{v\mid \exists i\in [k],s.t.\| v\| _2 = 1 and v is an eigenvector of S_i and \| S_iv\| _2\geqslant \sqrt{\delta}\right\}_{i\leqslant k}.$

Then, for every unit vector  $v \in V_{\mathrm{inf}}$ ,  $\| v - \Pi_V v \|_2^2 \leqslant 20 \delta^{1/4} \Delta / \alpha^2$ , where  $\Pi_V v$  is the projection of  $v$  onto  $V$ .

Proof. From Fact 2.6, we have that  $\mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})] = \sum_{i\in [k]}w_i\mathbb{E}[h_m(G_i)]$ , where  $G_{i} = \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$ , and since  $\| \mu_i\| _2\leqslant 2 / \sqrt{\alpha}$  and  $\| \Sigma_{i} - I\|_{F}\leqslant \Delta$ , it follows that  $\| \mathbb{E}[h_m(\mathcal{M})]\| _F^2\leqslant O(m(1 + 1 / \alpha +\Delta))^{4m}$ . From Proposition 3.3, we know that

$$
\left\| \hat {T} _ {m} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant 2 \left\| \hat {T} _ {m} - \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) ] \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} + 2 \left\| \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) ] \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant \eta^ {2} + \mathcal {O} (m (1 + 1 / \alpha + \Delta)) ^ {4 m}.
$$

Thus, the number of singular vectors of the  $d \times d^{m-1}$  flattening of  $\hat{T}_m$  with a singular value  $\geqslant \lambda$  is at most  $(\eta^2 + O(m(1 + 1/\alpha + \Delta))^{4m}) / \lambda^2$ . Summing up this bound for all  $m \in [4k]$ , yields the claimed upper bound on  $\dim(V)$ .

For the second part, we will first bound  $\langle u,v\rangle$  for any unit vector  $u$  orthogonal to the subspace  $V$ . Towards this, observe that since  $u$  is orthogonal to  $V$  and  $\| u\| _2 = 1$ , we have

$$
\left\| u \cdot \mathbb {E} [ h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) ] \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \left\| u \hat {T} _ {m} \right\| _ {F} + \left\| \hat {T} _ {m} - \mathbb {E} h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \lambda + \eta \leqslant 2 \lambda ,
$$

where  $u \cdot \mathbb{E} \left[ h_m(\mathcal{M}) \right]$  is a matrix-vector product of  $u$  with a  $d \times d^{m-1}$  flattening of  $\mathbb{E} \left[ h_m(\mathcal{M}) \right]$ . For  $\delta = 2\lambda^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$ , applying Lemma 3.10 yields that

$$
\left\langle \mu_ {i}, u \right\rangle^ {2} + \| S _ {i} u \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leqslant \delta^ {2} + 2 0 \delta \Delta / \alpha \leqslant 2 0 \delta \Delta / \alpha^ {2}. \tag {3.27}
$$

Now, if  $v$  is one of the  $\mu_i$ 's, then we immediately get from Equation 3.27 that  $\langle v, u \rangle^2 \leqslant 20\delta \Delta / \alpha^2$ . Similarly, note that if  $v$  is a unit length eigenvector of  $S_i$  satisfying  $\| S_i v \|_2^2 \geqslant \sqrt{\delta}$ , then,

$$
\left\langle u, v \right\rangle^ {2} = \frac {1}{\| S _ {i} v \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \left\langle u, S _ {i} v \right\rangle^ {2} = \frac {1}{\| S _ {i} v \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \left\langle S _ {i} u, v \right\rangle^ {2} \leqslant \frac {\| S _ {i} u \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\| S _ {i} v \| _ {2} ^ {2}}.
$$

In both cases, setting  $u = (v - \Pi_V v) / \| v - \Pi_V v \|_2$  completes the proof.

We can now complete the proof of Proposition 3.5:

Proof of Proposition 3.5. We know that  $\hat{S}_i - P_i - S_i$  is a symmetric, rank- $k'$  matrix such that  $k' = O(k^2)$ , described by the eigenvalue decomposition  $\sum_{i=1}^{k'} \tau_i v_i v_i^\top$ , where  $v_i$ 's are the eigenvectors and  $\tau_i$ 's are the corresponding eigenvalues. Since  $\|S_i\|_F \leqslant \Delta$  and

$$
\left\| \hat {S} _ {i} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \| P _ {i} \| _ {F} + \| Q _ {i} \| _ {F} + \| S _ {i} \| _ {F} \leqslant O \bigg (\sqrt {\eta / \alpha} \bigg) + O \bigg (\frac {1 + \Delta^ {2}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha^ {3}} \bigg) + \Delta = O \bigg (\frac {1 + \Delta^ {2}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha^ {3}} \bigg),
$$

we have that the number of singular values of  $\hat{S}_i$  that exceed  $\delta^{1/4}$  is at most  $O\left(\frac{1 + \Delta^2}{\sqrt{\eta} \alpha^3 \sqrt{\delta}}\right)$ . Recall that from Lemma 3.11 it follows that the dimension of the subspace  $V$  is at most  $k^{O(k)} O(1 + 1/\alpha + \Delta)^{4k}/\lambda^2$ . Thus, the dimension of  $V'$  is at most

$$
k ^ {O (k)} O \left(\frac {(1 + 1 / \alpha + \Delta) ^ {4 k}}{\lambda^ {2}}\right) + O \left(\frac {1 + \Delta^ {2}}{\sqrt {\eta} \alpha^ {3} \sqrt {\delta}}\right) = O \left(\frac {k ^ {O (k)} \left(1 + \frac {1}{\alpha} + \Delta\right) ^ {4 k + 5}}{\eta^ {2}}\right).
$$

Since  $V'$  contains  $V$  constructed in Lemma 3.11, we immediately obtain that for every  $\mu_i$ ,  $\left\| \mu_i - \Pi_{V'} \mu_i \right\|_2^2 \leqslant \frac{20}{\alpha^2} \sqrt{\delta} \Delta$ .

Next, let  $u$  be a unit vector orthogonal to  $V'$ . Then, since  $V'$  contains the  $V$  described in Lemma 3.11, we know that  $\| S_i u \|_2^2 \leqslant \frac{20}{\alpha^2} \sqrt{\delta} \Delta$ . Similarly, since  $V'$  contains all eigenvectors of  $\hat{S}_i$  with singular values exceeding  $\delta^{1/4}$ , we know that  $\left\| \hat{S}_i u \right\|_2^2 \leqslant \delta^{1/2}$ . Thus, we can conclude that  $\left\| (\hat{S}_i - S_i) u \right\|_2^2 \leqslant \frac{100}{\alpha^2} \sqrt{\delta} \Delta$ . Let  $Q_i = \sum_{j=1}^{k'} \tau_j v_j v_j^\top$  with orthonormal  $v_j \in \mathbb{R}^d$ . We know such  $\tau_j'$ 's and  $v_j'$ 's exist because of the upper bound on  $\text{rank}(Q_i)$ . Therefore, for any  $j$ ,  $|v_j^\top (\hat{S}_i - S_i) u| \leqslant \frac{10}{\alpha} \delta^{1/4} \Delta^{1/2}$ . On the other hand, for any  $j$ , we have that

$$
v _ {j} ^ {\top} (\hat {S} _ {i} - S _ {i}) u \geqslant \langle v _ {j}, u \rangle \tau_ {j} - \| P _ {i} \| _ {F} = \langle v _ {j}, u \rangle \tau_ {j} - O (\sqrt {\eta}).
$$

Combining the two bounds above, yields that whenever  $\tau_{j}\geqslant \delta^{1 / 4}$

$$
| \langle v _ {j}, u \rangle | \leqslant O (\sqrt {\eta} / \tau_ {j}) + \frac {1 0}{\alpha \tau_ {j}} \delta^ {1 / 4} \Delta^ {1 / 2} \leqslant \frac {1 0}{\alpha} \delta^ {1 / 2} \Delta^ {1 / 2}.
$$

Thus, the matrix  $\hat{Q}_i = \sum_{j=1}^{k'} \tau_j \Pi_{V'} v_j (\Pi_{V'} v_j)^\top$  has its range space in  $V'$  and satisfies

$$
\left\| \hat {Q} _ {i} - Q _ {i} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \mathcal {O} \Big (k ^ {2} \delta^ {1 / 4} \Big) + \mathcal {O} \Big (\frac {k ^ {2}}{\alpha} \delta^ {1 / 2} \Delta^ {1 / 2} \Big) = \mathcal {O} \Big (\frac {k ^ {2}}{\alpha} \delta^ {1 / 2} \Delta^ {1 / 2} \Big).
$$

# 3.6 Parameter vs Moment Distance for Gaussian Mixtures

In this subsection, we prove Lemma 3.9. To that end, we will use the following two results; the second one is from [MV10].

Lemma 3.12. Suppose  $\mathcal{N}(\mu_1,\sigma_1^2)$  and  $\mathcal{N}(\mu_2,\sigma_2^2)$  are univariate Gaussians with  $|\mu_i|,|\sigma_i| \leqslant D$ , for some  $D \in \mathbb{R}_+$ . If  $|\mu_1 - \mu_2| + |\sigma_1^2 - \sigma_2^2| \leqslant \beta$ , then the distance between raw moments of two Gaussians is

$$
\left| M _ {j} (\mathcal {N} (\mu_ {1}, \sigma_ {1} ^ {2})) - M _ {j} (\mathcal {N} (\mu_ {2}, \sigma_ {2} ^ {2})) \right| \leqslant (j + 1)! D ^ {j - 1} \beta .
$$

Proof. By Proposition 2.3, the  $j$ -th raw moment of a Gaussian  $\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$  is a sum of monomials in  $\mu$  and  $\sigma^2$  of degree  $j$ . There are at most  $(j + 1)!$  terms in the polynomial. Thus, changing the mean or the variance by at most  $\beta$  will change the  $j$ -th moment by at most  $(j + 1)!D^{j - 1}\beta$ .

Theorem 3.13. ([MV10]) Let  $F, F'$  be two univariate mixtures of Gaussians:  $F = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \sigma_i^2)$  and  $F' = \sum_{i=1}^{k'} w_i' \mathcal{N}(\mu_i', \sigma_i'^2)$ . There is a constant  $c > 0$  such that, for any  $\beta < c$ , if  $F, F'$  satisfy:

1.  $w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\in [\beta ,1]$  
2.  $|\mu_i|, |\mu_i'| \leqslant 1 / \beta$  
3.  $|\mu_i - \mu_{i'}| + |\sigma_i^2 -\sigma_{i'}^2 |\geqslant \beta$  and  $|\mu_i' - \mu_{i'}'| + |\sigma_i'^2 -\sigma_{i'}'^2 |\geqslant \beta$  for all  $i\neq i'$  
4.  $\beta \leqslant \min_{\pi}\sum_{i}\left(|w_{i} - w^{\prime}_{\pi (i)}| + |\mu_{i} - \mu^{\prime}_{\pi (i)}| + |\sigma^{2} - \sigma^{\prime}_{\pi (i)}{}^{2}|\right)$ , where the minimization is taken over all mappings  $\pi :\{1,\ldots ,k\} \to \{1,\ldots ,k'\}$

then

$$
\max  _ {j \in [ 2 (k + k ^ {\prime} - 1) ]} | M _ {j} (F) - M _ {j} (F ^ {\prime}) | \geqslant \beta^ {O (k)}.
$$

We are now ready to complete the proof of Lemma 3.9.

Proof of Lemma 3.9. We proceed via induction on  $k$ . Consider the base case, i.e.,  $k = 1$ . Then, either  $\left|\mu_1\right| \geqslant \beta /2$  or  $|\sigma_{1} - 1| \geqslant \beta /2$ , and thus the first or second moment differ by at least  $\beta^2 /4$ . Let the inductive hypothesis be that Lemma 3.9 holds for at most  $k$  components.

Consider the case where  $|\mu_i - \mu_{i'}| + |\sigma_i^2 -\sigma_{i'}^2 |\geqslant \beta^{C^k k!}$  for all pairs of components  $i,i^{\prime}\in [k]$ . Then, by Theorem 3.13, we have that

$$
