Then, rescaling the instance by  $O(\varepsilon / d^2)$  and applying Lemma 8.8 with  $\mathcal{D}_1 = \mathcal{M}$ ,  $\mathcal{D}_2 = \bar{\mathcal{M}}$ , input samples  $X$  and accuracy parameter  $\tau = (\varepsilon / d)^{c_2}$ , for a large enough constant  $c_2$ , it follows that with probability at least  $1 - 0.99 - O(f(d, \varepsilon_1) \cdot (\varepsilon / d)^{c_3})$ , for a fixed constant  $c_3$ , the resulting list of numbers is  $\tau$ -close to that obtained by running PARTITION PURSUIT on a set of  $n$  samples from  $\bar{\mathcal{M}}$ . Since  $\bar{\mathcal{M}}$  is  $\varepsilon_1$ -statistically learnable, it follows from Theorem 8.5 that with probability at least 9/10, PARTITION PURSUIT will output an  $O(\varepsilon_1)$ -correct sub-division  $\hat{\mathcal{M}}$ .

Proof of Theorem 8.2. Recall, by part (1) of Proposition 3.5, the dimension of the subspace  $V_{\hat{S}}'$  is  $m = \dim(V_{\hat{S}}') = O\left(\frac{(k(1 + \Delta + 1 / \alpha))^{4k + 5}}{\eta^2}\right)$  and let  $\varepsilon_1 = \sqrt{\Delta} \delta^{1/4} / \alpha$ . Let  $c_{mv}$  be a fixed constant such that  $(m / \varepsilon_1)^{c_{mv}}$  samples suffice for applying Theorem 8.5. Further, observe in the fresh sample  $Y$ , the probability that any given sample is corrupted is  $\varepsilon$ . Let  $\zeta$  be the event that a random subset of  $(m / \varepsilon_1)^{c_{mv}}$  samples from  $Z$  does not contain any corrupted points. Then, the event  $\zeta$  holds with probability at least  $(1 - \varepsilon)^{(m / \varepsilon_1)^{c_{mv}}}$ . Conditioning on  $\zeta$  and running step 4 of Algorithm 8.6, it follows from Proposition 8.7 that we recover  $O(\varepsilon_1)$ -accurate estimates to the parameters of  $\mathcal{M}$  in the subspace, i.e.  $\| U^\top \mu_i - \hat{\mu}_i \|_2 \leqslant O(\varepsilon_1)$  and  $\| U^\top \Sigma_i U - \hat{\Sigma}_i \|_F \leqslant O(\varepsilon_1)$ . Since we repeat the above for  $\ell'$  candidate subspaces in  $L'$ , the probability over all probability of success is  $(1 - \varepsilon)^{(m / \varepsilon_1)^{c_{mv}} \cdot \ell'}$ .

By part (2) in Proposition 3.5, there is a vector  $\mu_i' \in V_{\hat{S}}'$  such that  $\left\| \mu_i - \mu_i' \right\| \leqslant \frac{20}{\alpha} \delta^{1/4} \Delta^{1/2}$  where  $\delta = 2\eta^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$  and  $\eta = O(4k(1 + 1/\alpha + \Delta)^{4k} \sqrt{\varepsilon_1})$ . Let  $\hat{P} = U U^\top$  be a projection matrix where the columns of  $Q$  span  $V_{\hat{S}}'$  and let  $Q^\top \mu_i$  be the projection of the true means to the corresponding

subspace. Then,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| U \hat {\mu} _ {i} - \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {2} \leqslant \left\| U \hat {\mu} _ {i} - \mu_ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \mu_ {i} ^ {\prime} - \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {2} \\ \leqslant \left\| U \hat {\mu} _ {i} - P (\mu_ {i} ^ {\prime} - \mu_ {i} + \mu_ {i}) \right\| _ {2} + O \left(\frac {\sqrt {\Delta} \delta^ {1 / 4}}{\alpha}\right) \\ \leqslant \left\| \hat {\mu} _ {i} - U ^ {\top} \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {2} + O \left(\frac {\sqrt {\Delta} \delta^ {1 / 4}}{\alpha}\right) \\ \leqslant O \left(\frac {\sqrt {\Delta} \delta^ {1 / 4}}{\alpha}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third inequality follows from observing that  $U^{\top}\mu_{i}$  is the true mean in the low dimensional subspace and applying Proposition 8.7.

By Proposition 3.4, there exists  $\hat{S} \in L'$  such that  $\hat{S} - (\Sigma_i - I) = P_i + Q_i$  where  $\|P_i\|_F = O(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha})$ . Again by part (3) in Proposition 3.5, there exists a symmetric matrix  $Q_i' \in V_{\hat{S}}' \times V_{\hat{S}}'$  such that  $\left\|Q_i - Q_i'\right\|_F \leqslant O\left(\frac{k^2}{\alpha}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}\right)$ . We also know that in the subspace spanned by  $V_{\hat{S}}'$ ,  $\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_i - U^\top\Sigma_iU\right\|_F^2 \leqslant \mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon_2)$ . Recall, Algorithm 8.6 outputs the following estimate:  $\hat{M} = U\hat{\Sigma}U^\top + (I + \hat{S}) - \hat{P}(I + \hat{S})\hat{P}$ . Observe, for any matrix  $M$  and projection matrix  $P$ ,  $M = PMP + (I - P)M(I - P) + PM(I - P) + (I - P)MP$ . Then,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \underbrace {\left\| \hat {P} \left(\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}\right) \hat {P} \right\| _ {F}} _ {(1)} + \underbrace {\left\| (I - \hat {P}) (\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F}} _ {(2)} \\ + \underbrace {\left\| \hat {P} \left(\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}\right) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F}} _ {(3)} + \underbrace {\left\| (I - \hat {P}) (\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}) \hat {P} \right\| _ {F}} _ {(4)} \tag {8.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

We bound each of the terms above. Since  $\hat{P} (I + S - P(I + S)P)\hat{P} = 0$ , we can bound term (1) as follows

$$
\left\| \hat {P} \left(\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}\right) \hat {P} \right\| _ {F} = \left\| \hat {P} \Sigma_ {i} \hat {P} - \hat {P} U \hat {\Sigma} _ {i} U ^ {\top} \hat {P} \right\| _ {F} = \left\| U ^ {\top} \Sigma_ {i} U - \hat {\Sigma} _ {i} \right\| \leqslant O \left(\frac {\sqrt {\Delta} \delta^ {1 / 4}}{\alpha}\right) \tag {8.3}
$$

Similarly, since  $\left(I - \hat{P}\right)\left(U\hat{\Sigma}_iU^\top\right)\left(I - \hat{P}\right) = 0$  and  $\Sigma_i = I + \hat{S} - P_i - Q_i$ , we can bound term (2) as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(I - \hat {P}\right) \left(\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}\right) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} = \left\| \left(I - \hat {P}\right) \left(\Sigma_ {i} - (I + \hat {S})\right) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} \\ \leqslant \left\| (I - \hat {P}) (\Sigma_ {i} - (I + \hat {S} - Q _ {i})) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} + \left\| (I - \hat {P}) Q _ {i} (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} \\ \leqslant \| P _ {i} \| _ {F} ^ {2} + \left\| (I - \hat {P}) \left(Q _ {i} - Q _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} + \left\| (I - \hat {P}) Q _ {i} ^ {\prime} (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} \\ \leqslant O \left(\sqrt {\frac {\eta}{\alpha}} + \frac {k ^ {2} \delta^ {1 / 4} \Delta^ {1 / 2}}{\alpha}\right) \tag {8.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

Next, we bound term (3). Observe,  $\hat{P}\left(U\hat{\Sigma}_iU^\top\right)\left(I - \hat{P}\right) = 0$  and  $\hat{P}(I + S)\hat{P}(I - \hat{P}) = 0$ . Thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \hat {P} (\Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M}) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} = \left\| \hat {P} (\Sigma_ {i} - (I + \hat {S})) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} \\ = \left\| \hat {P} \left(P _ {i} + Q _ {i}\right) (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} \\ \leqslant \left\| \hat {P} P _ {i} (I - \hat {P}) \right\| _ {F} \tag {8.5} \\ \leqslant O \left(\sqrt {\frac {\eta}{\alpha}}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Observe, term (4) follows from a similar argument. Combining equations (8.3), (8.4),(8.5) and substituting back into (8.2) we can conclude

$$
\left\| \Sigma_ {i} - \hat {M} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant O \left(\sqrt {\frac {\eta}{\alpha}} + \frac {k ^ {2} \delta^ {1 / 4} \Delta^ {1 / 2}}{\alpha}\right)
$$

The size of  $L'$  is  $\ell' = O\left(\log k\left(\eta / \left(k^5\left(\Delta^4 + 1/\alpha^4\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}\right)$  and since we add a single tuple of  $k$  means and covariances for each subspace in  $L'$ , the list  $L$  has the same size. The running time is poly  $(|Y|, |L|, d^k, m, 1/\varepsilon_1)$  concluding the proof.

# 8.2 Proof of Theorem 8.1

Since we have all the main ingredients: the tensor decomposition algorithm recovering a polynomial size of list (Theorem 8.2), the upgraded partial clustering algorithm with high probability of success (Theorem 7.1) and the spectral separation algorithm of thin components (Lemma 5.1), we can now complete the proof of Theorem 8.1.

The algorithm establishing Theorem 8.1 is almost the same as Algorithm 6.3. The only difference is we will replace Algorithm 4.4 by Algorithm 7.2 and replace Algorithm 3.2 by Algorithm 8.6. The following two lemmas show that by modifying the parameters slightly and applying the upgraded partial clustering and tensor decomposition algorithms, we can have the same conclusions as in Lemma 6.8 and Lemma 6.9 with a polynomial success probability. Then the proof of Theorem 8.1 is exactly the same as the proof of Theorem 6.1 in Section 6.2 except for the use of Lemma 8.10 and Lemma 8.11 instead of Lemma 6.8 and Lemma 6.9.

Lemma 8.10 (Non-negligible Weight and Covariance Separation). Given  $0 < \varepsilon < 1 / k^{k^{O(k^2)}}$  and  $k\in \mathbb{N}$  let  $\alpha = \varepsilon^{1 / (45C^{k + 1}(k + 1)!)}$

Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$  with  $G_i = \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians with mixture covariance  $\Sigma$  such that  $w_i \geqslant \alpha$  for all  $i \in [k]$  and there exist  $i, j \in [k]$  such that  $\left\| \Sigma^{\dagger/2} (\Sigma_i - \Sigma_j) \Sigma^{\dagger/2} \right\|_F^2 > 1/\alpha^5$ . Further, let  $X$  be a set of points satisfying Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  for some parameters  $\gamma \leqslant \varepsilon d^{-8k} k^{-Ck}$ , for a sufficiently large constant  $C$ , and  $t \geqslant 8k$ . Let  $Y$  be an  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted version of  $X$  of size  $n \geqslant n_0 = (dk)^{\Omega(1)}/\varepsilon$ . Algorithm 7.2 partitions  $Y$  into  $Y_1, Y_2$  in time  $n^{\mathrm{O}(1)}$  such that with probability at least  $2^{-\mathrm{O}(k)} (1 - \mathrm{O}(\alpha))$  there is a non-trivial partition of  $[k]$  into  $Q_1 \cup Q_2$  so that letting  $\mathcal{M}_j$  be a distribution proportional to

\(\sum_{i \in Q_j} w_i G_i\) and \(W_j = \sum_{i \in Q_j} w_i\), then \(Y_j\) is an \(\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!\)}\right)\)-corrupted version of \(\bigcup_{i \in Q_j} X_i\) satisfying Condition 2.47 with respect to \(\mathcal{M}\) with parameters \((O(k\gamma / W_j), t)\).

Proof. We run Algorithm 7.2 with sample set  $Y$ , number of components  $k$ , the fraction of outliers  $\varepsilon$  and the accuracy parameter  $\eta$ . Since  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47, we can set  $t = 10, \beta = (k^2 t^4 \alpha)^{t/2} = O_k(\alpha^5)$  and  $\eta = \alpha^2 \gg \sqrt{\varepsilon / \alpha}$  in Theorem 7.1. Then, by assumption, there exist  $i, j$  such that

$$
\left\| \Sigma^ {\dagger / 2} \left(\Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma_ {j}\right) \Sigma^ {\dagger / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} > \frac {1}{\alpha^ {5}} = \Omega \left(\frac {k ^ {2} t ^ {4}}{\beta^ {2 / t} \alpha^ {4}}\right).
$$

We observe that we also satisfy the other preconditions for Theorem 7.1, since  $n \geqslant (dk) / {}^{\Omega(1)} / \varepsilon$ .

Then, Theorem 7.1 implies that with probability at least  $2^{-O(k)}(1 - O(\eta / \alpha - \sqrt{\eta})) = 2^{-O(k)}(1 - O(\alpha))$ , the set  $Y$  is partitioned in two sets  $Y_{1}$  and  $Y_{2}$  such that there is a non-trivial partition of  $[k]$  into  $Q_{1} \cup Q_{2}$  so that letting  $\mathcal{M}_{j}$  be a distribution proportional to  $\sum_{i \in Q_{j}} w_{i} G_{i}$  and  $W_{j} = \sum_{i \in Q_{j}} w_{i}$ , then  $Y_{j}$  is an  $\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)$ -corrupted version of  $\bigcup_{i \in Q_{j}} X_{i}$ . By Lemma 2.50,  $\bigcup_{i \in Q_{j}} X_{i}$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\mathcal{O}(k\gamma / W_{j}), t)$ .

![](images/7386b60bb8d078a50df8474057327f0ba6caa8fec9bc13b50ff5e6ea64a58c2c.jpg)

When the mixture is not covariance separated and nearly isotropic, we can obtain a small list of hypotheses such that one of them is close to the true parameters, via tensor decomposition.

Lemma 8.11 (Mixture is List-decodable). Given  $0 < \varepsilon < 1 / k^{k^{O(k^2)}}$  let  $\alpha = \varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$ . Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$  with  $G_i = \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians with mixture mean  $\mu$  and mixture covariance  $\Sigma$ , such that  $\left\| \mu \right\|_2 \leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}\right)$ ,  $\left\| \Sigma - I \right\|_F \leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right)$ ,  $w_i \geqslant \alpha$  for all  $i \in [k]$ , and  $\left\| \Sigma_i - \Sigma_j \right\|_F^2 \leqslant 1/\alpha^5$  for any pair of components, and let  $X$  be a set of points satisfying Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  for some parameters  $\gamma = \varepsilon d^{-8k} k^{-Ck}$ , for a sufficiently large constant  $C$ , and  $t = 8k$ . Let  $Y$  be an  $\varepsilon$ -corrupted version of  $X$  of size  $n$ , Algorithm 8.6 outputs a list  $L$  of hypotheses of size  $O((1/\varepsilon)^{4k^2})$  in time poly  $(|L|, n)$  such that if we choose a hypothesis  $\{\hat{\mu}_i, \hat{\Sigma}_i\}_{i \in [k]}$  uniformly at random,  $\left\| \mu_i - \hat{\mu}_i \right\|_2 \leqslant O\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)$  and  $\left\| \Sigma_i - \hat{\Sigma}_i \right\|_F \leqslant O\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)$  for all  $i$  with probability at least  $O(\varepsilon^{4k^2})$ .

Proof. Recall we run Algorithm 8.6 on the samples  $Y$ , the number of clusters  $k$ , the fraction of outliers  $\varepsilon$  and the minimum weight  $\alpha = \varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}$ . Next, we show that the preconditions of Theorem 8.2 are satisfied. First, the upper bounds on  $\left\| \mu \right\|_2$  and  $\left\| \Sigma - I \right\|_F$  imply  $\sum_{i \in k} w_i \left( \Sigma_i + \mu_i \mu_i^\top \right) = \Sigma + \mu \mu^\top \preceq (1 + O(\sqrt{\varepsilon} / \alpha)) I$ . Since the LHS is a conic combination of PSD matrices, it follows that for all  $i \in [k]$ ,  $\mu_i \mu_i^\top \preceq \frac{1}{\alpha} \left( 1 + O(\sqrt{\varepsilon} / \alpha) \right) I$ , and thus  $\left\| \mu_i \mu_i^\top \right\|_F \leqslant \frac{2}{\alpha}$ . Next, we can write:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \Sigma_ {i} - I \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \left\| \Sigma_ {i} - (\Sigma + \mu \mu^ {\top}) \right\| _ {F} + \left\| \Sigma - I \right\| _ {F} + \left\| \mu \mu^ {\top} \right\| _ {F} \\ = \left\| \Sigma_ {i} - \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} w _ {j} \left(\Sigma_ {j} + \mu_ {j} \mu_ {j} ^ {\top}\right) \right\| _ {F} + \frac {\sqrt {\varepsilon} k}{\alpha} + \frac {\varepsilon}{\alpha} \\ \leqslant \left\| \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} w _ {j} (\Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma_ {j}) \right\| _ {F} + \frac {2}{\alpha} + \frac {\sqrt {\varepsilon} k}{\alpha} + \frac {\varepsilon}{\alpha} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\leqslant \frac {2}{\alpha^ {5 / 2}} ,
$$

where the first and the third inequalities follow from the triangle inequality and the upper bound on  $\left\| \mu_i \mu_i^\top \right\|_F$ , and the last inequality follows from the assumption that  $\left\| \Sigma_i - \Sigma_j \right\|_F^2 \leqslant 1 / \alpha^5$  for every pair of covariances  $\Sigma_i, \Sigma_j$ . So, we can set  $\Delta = 2\alpha^{-5/2}$  in Theorem 8.2. Then, given the definition of  $\alpha$ , we have that

$$
\eta = 2 k ^ {4 k} O (1 + \Delta / \alpha) ^ {4 k} \sqrt {\varepsilon} = O \Big (\varepsilon^ {2 / 5} \Big)
$$

and  $1 / \varepsilon^2 \geqslant \log(1 / \eta)(k + 1 / \alpha + \Delta)^{4k + 5} / \eta^2$ . Therefore, Algorithm 8.6 outputs a list  $L$  of hypotheses such that  $|L| = \exp(1 / \varepsilon^2)$ , and with probability at least 0.99,  $L$  contains a hypothesis that satisfies the following: for all  $i \in [k]$ ,

$$
\left\| \hat {\mu} _ {i} - \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {2} = O \left(\frac {\Delta^ {1 / 2}}{\alpha}\right) \eta^ {G (k)} = O \left(\varepsilon^ {- 1 / (2 0 C ^ {k + 1} (k + 1)!)} \cdot \varepsilon^ {1 / (1 0 C ^ {k + 1} (k + 1)!)}\right) = O \left(\varepsilon^ {1 / (2 0 C ^ {k + 1} (k + 1)!)}\right) \text {a n d} \tag {8.6}
$$

$$
\left\| \hat {\Sigma} _ {i} - \Sigma_ {i} \right\| _ {F} = O \big (k ^ {4} \big) \frac {\Delta^ {1 / 2}}{\alpha} \eta^ {G (k)} = O \Big (\varepsilon^ {1 / (2 0 C ^ {k + 1} (k + 1) !)} \Big).
$$

Then if we choose a hypothesis in  $L$  uniformly at random, the probability that we choose the hypothesis satisfying (6.2) is at least  $1 / |L| = \exp \left(-1 / \varepsilon^2\right)$ .

# 9 Robust Parameter Recovery: Proof of Theorem 1.6

In order to show that our algorithm recovers the individual components and the parameters, we will prove the following identifiability theorem. Without any assumption on the mixtures, it is impossible to distinguish components within  $\varepsilon$  total variation distance with  $\varepsilon$ -fraction of noise. So given two mixtures of Gaussians with  $\varepsilon$  total variation distance, the theorem shows that there exist two partitions of components of the two mixtures respectively such that any two components in the matched pair are poly( $\varepsilon$ )-close in total variation distance.

Theorem 9.1 (Identifiability). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k_1} w_i G_i$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k_2} w_i' G_i'$  be two mixtures of Gaussians such that  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \mathcal{M}') \leqslant \varepsilon$ . Then there exists a partition of  $[k_1]$  into sets  $R_0, R_1, \ldots, R_\ell$  and a partition of  $[k_2]$  into sets  $S_0, S_1, \ldots, S_\ell$  such that

1. Let  $W_{i} = \sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}$  for  $i = 0,1,\ldots ,k_{1}$ ,  $W_{i}^{\prime} = \sum_{j\in S_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}$  for  $i = 0,1,\ldots ,k_{2}$ . Then for all  $i\in [\ell ]$

$$
\left| W _ {i} - W _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right| \leqslant \operatorname {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon)
$$

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (G _ {j}, G _ {j ^ {\prime}} ^ {\prime}) \leqslant \mathrm {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon) \quad \forall j \in R _ {i}, j ^ {\prime} \in S _ {i}
$$

2.  $W_0, W_0' \leqslant \mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$ .

Corollary 9.2. There is an algorithm with the following behavior: Given  $\varepsilon >0$  and a multiset of  $n = d^{O(k)}\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon)$  samples from a distribution  $F$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  such that  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(F,\mathcal{M})\leqslant \varepsilon$ , for an unknown target  $k$ -GMM  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i = 1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$ , the algorithm runs in time  $d^{O(k)}\mathrm{poly}_k(1 / \varepsilon)$  and outputs a  $k^{\prime}$ -GMM hypothesis  $\widehat{\mathcal{M}} = \sum_{i = 1}^{k^{\prime}}\widehat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}(\widehat{\mu}_{i},\widehat{\Sigma}_{i})$  with  $k^{\prime}\leqslant k$  such that with high probability there exists a partition of  $[k]$  into  $k^{\prime} + 1$  sets  $R_0,R_1,\ldots ,R_{k'}$  such that

1. Let  $W_{i} = \sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}$ . Then for all  $i\in [k^{\prime}]$

$$
\left| W _ {i} - \hat {w} _ {i} \right| \leqslant \operatorname {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon)
$$

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} \left(\mathcal {N} \left(\mu_ {j}, \Sigma_ {j}\right), \mathcal {N} \left(\widehat {\mu_ {i}}, \widehat {\Sigma_ {i}}\right)\right) \leqslant \operatorname {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon) \quad \forall j \in R _ {i}
$$

2. The sum of weights of exceptional components in  $R_0$  is at most  $\mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$ .

Parameter estimation is implied by TV distance for individual Gaussians (in relative Frobenius norm). The corollary follows immediately from the identifiability theorem.

Outline of Proof. The first step is to deal with the components in  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  with small weights. We will construct  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}, \tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$  by removing components with small weights. If we prove the statement on  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}, \tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$ , we can then deduce the theorem in the general case with worse, but still polynomial dependencies on  $\varepsilon$ . The second step is a partial clustering, after which the components within each cluster have TV distance bounded by  $1 - \mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon)$ . We prove this lemma in a separate section. After that we modify the parameters slightly so that the resulting parameters for different components are either identical or have a minimum separation. After this, we can use a lemma from [LM20] that provides a 1-1 mapping between the components of two such mixtures with small TV distance such that the mapped pairs have small TV distance.

Distance between Gaussians. We use the following facts for Gaussian distributions.

Lemma 9.3 (Frobenius Distance to TV Distance). Suppose  $N(\mu_1, \Sigma_1), N(\mu_2, \Sigma_2)$  are Gaussians with  $\| \mu_1 - \mu_2 \|_2 \leqslant \delta$  and  $\| \Sigma_1 - \Sigma_2 \|_F \leqslant \delta$ . If the eigenvalues of  $\Sigma_1$  and  $\Sigma_2$  are at least  $\lambda > 0$ , then  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(N(\mu_1, \Sigma_1), N(\mu_2, \Sigma_2)) = O(\delta / \lambda)$ .

Lemma 9.4 (Lemma 5.4 in [LM20]). Let  $\mathcal{M}$  be a mixture of  $k$  Gaussians that is connected if we draw edges between all components  $i, j$  in  $\mathcal{M}$  such that  $d_{\top \vee}(G_i, G_j) \leqslant 1 - \delta$ . Let  $\Sigma$  be the covariance matrix of  $\mathcal{M}$ . Then for any components  $\Sigma_i$  of the mixture

1.  $\Sigma_{i}\succeq \mathrm{poly}_{k}(\delta)\Sigma$  
2.  $\left\| \Sigma^{-1 / 2}(\Sigma -\Sigma_{i})\Sigma^{-1 / 2}\right\|_{F}\leqslant \mathrm{poly}_{k}(\delta)^{-1}.$

The proof is identical to Lemma 5.4 in [LM20]. The only difference is that in [LM20] the authors assume that the minimal weight of  $\mathcal{M}$  is at least  $\delta$  and TV distance between any pair of components is at least  $\delta$  but here we do not need these two assumptions, which does not affect the proof.

Fact 9.5 (Claim 3.9 in [LM20]). Let  $\partial$  denote the differential operator with respect to  $y$ . If

$$
f (y) = P (y, X) \exp \left(a (X) y + \frac {1}{2} b (X) y ^ {2}\right)
$$

where  $P$  is a polynomial in  $y$  of degree  $k$  (whose coefficients are polynomials in  $X$ ) and  $a(X), b(X)$  are polynomials in  $X$  then

$$
(\partial - (a (X) + y b (X))) f (y) = Q (y, X) \exp \left(a (X) y + \frac {1}{2} b (X) y ^ {2}\right)
$$

where  $Q$  is a polynomial in  $y$  with degree exactly  $k - 1$  whose leading coefficient is  $k$  times the leading coefficient of  $P$ .

Fact 9.6 (Corollary 3.10 in [LM20]). Let  $\partial$  denote the differential operator with respect to  $y$ . If

$$
f (y) = P (y, X) \exp \left(a (X) y + \frac {1}{2} b (X) y ^ {2}\right)
$$

where  $P$  is a polynomial in  $y$  of degree  $k$  then

$$
\left. (\partial - (a (X) + y b (X))) ^ {k + 1} f (y) = 0. \right.
$$

Fact 9.7 (Claim 3.11 in [LM20]). Let  $\partial$  denote the differential operator with respect to  $y$ . If

$$
f (y) = P (y, X) \exp \left(a (X) y + \frac {1}{2} b (X) y ^ {2}\right)
$$

where  $P$  is a polynomial in  $y$  of degree  $k$ . Let the leading coefficient of  $P$  (viewed as a polynomial in  $y$ ) be  $L(X)$ . Let  $c(X)$  be a linear polynomial in  $X$  and  $d(X)$  be a quadratic polynomial in  $X$  such that  $\{a(X), b(X)\} \neq \{c(X), d(X)\}$ . If  $b(X) \neq d(X)$  then

$$
\left. \left(\partial - (c (X) + y d (X))\right) ^ {k ^ {\prime}} f (y) = Q (y, X) \exp \left(a (X) y + \frac {1}{2} b (X) y ^ {2}\right) \right.
$$

where  $Q$  is a polynomial of degree  $k + k'$  in  $y$  with leading coefficient

$$
L (X) (b (X) - d (X)) ^ {k ^ {\prime}}
$$

and if  $b(X) = d(X)$  then

$$
\left(\partial - \left(c (X) + y d (X)\right)\right) ^ {k ^ {\prime}} f (y) = Q (y, X) \exp \left(a (X) y + \frac {1}{2} b (X) y ^ {2}\right)
$$

where  $Q$  is a polynomial of degree  $k$  in  $y$  with leading coefficient

$$
L (X) (a (X) - c (X)) ^ {k ^ {\prime}}.
$$

Lemma 9.8. Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k_1} w_i G_i$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k_2} w_i' G_i'$  be two mixtures of Gaussians such that  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \mathcal{M}') \leqslant \varepsilon$ . For any constant  $0 < c_1 < 1$ , there exists  $i \in [k_1 + k_2 + 1]$  such that  $w_j, w_{j'}' \notin [\varepsilon^{c_1^{i-1}}, \varepsilon^{c_1^i})$  for any  $j \in [k_1], j' \in [k_2]$ . Moreover, if

$$
\tilde {\mathcal {M}} = \frac {\sum_ {\{j : w _ {j} \geqslant \varepsilon^ {c _ {1} ^ {i}} \}} w _ {j} G _ {j}}{\sum_ {\{j : w _ {j} \geqslant \varepsilon^ {c _ {1} ^ {i}} \}} w _ {j}}
$$

$$
\tilde {\mathcal {M}} ^ {\prime} = \frac {\sum_ {\{j : w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \geqslant \varepsilon^ {c _ {1} ^ {i}} \}} w _ {j} ^ {\prime} G _ {j} ^ {\prime}}{\sum_ {\{j : w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \geqslant \varepsilon^ {c _ {1} ^ {i}} \}} w _ {j} ^ {\prime}}
$$

then  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i - 1}})$

Proof. We can see that  $\left[\varepsilon_{1}^{c_{1}^{i-1}}, \varepsilon_{1}^{c_{1}^{i}}\right)$  with  $i \in [k_{1} + k_{2} + 1]$  are  $k_{1} + k_{2} + 1$  disjoint intervals and  $w_{j}, w_{j'}'$  with  $j \in [k_{1}], j' \in [k_{2}]$  have at most  $k_{1} + k_{2}$  distinct values. So there is one interval containing no weights.

We then construct  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  by removing the small components in  $\mathcal{M}$ . The sum of weights removed is at most  $k\varepsilon^{c_1^{i-1}}$ . So  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \tilde{\mathcal{M}}) \leqslant k\varepsilon^{c_1^{i-1}}$ . Similarly, we have  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}', \tilde{\mathcal{M}}') \leqslant k\varepsilon^{c_1^{i-1}}$ . By the triangle inequality,

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}}, \tilde {\mathcal {M}} ^ {\prime}) \leqslant d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\mathcal {M}, \mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}) + d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\mathcal {M}, \tilde {\mathcal {M}}) + d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}, \tilde {\mathcal {M}} ^ {\prime}) \leqslant O _ {k} \left(\varepsilon_ {1} ^ {c ^ {i - 1}}\right).
$$

Lemma 9.8 shows that we can remove components with tiny weights in the mixtures. So in the following lemma, we will assume  $M$  and  $M'$  are Gaussian mixtures with minimal weights at least poly(ε). We will show that we can partition the union of components of two mixtures so that if we prove Theorem 9.1 for each part of the partition, we can combine them to prove Theorem 9.1 on the full mixtures.

Lemma 9.9. For any constant  $0 < c_{3} < 1$ , there exist  $c_{1}, c_{2} > 0$  that depend on  $k$  and  $c_{3}$ , such that if  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}} w_{i} G_{i}$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}} w_{i}' G_{i}'$  with  $k_{1}, k_{2} \leqslant k$ ,  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \mathcal{M}') \leqslant \varepsilon$  and  $w_{i}, w_{i}' \geqslant \varepsilon^{c_{1}}$  for all  $i$ , then there exists a partition of  $[k_{1}]$  into sets  $R_{1}, \ldots, R_{\ell}$  and a partition of  $[k_{2}]$  into sets  $S_{1}, \ldots, S_{\ell}$  such that

1. For all  $i \in [\ell]$ , let  $W_i = \sum_{j \in R_i} w_j, W_i' = \sum_{j \in S_i} w_j'$  be the sum of weights in each piece. Let  $\mathcal{M}_i = \frac{1}{W_i} \sum_{j \in R_i} w_j G_j, \mathcal{M}_i' = \frac{1}{W_i'} \sum_{j \in S_i} w_j' G_j'$  be the submixtures of Gaussians after partition. Then for all  $i \in [\ell]$ ,

$$
\left| W _ {i} - W _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right| \leqslant \operatorname {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon)
$$

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} \left(\mathcal {M} _ {i}, \mathcal {M} _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) \leqslant O _ {k} \left(\varepsilon^ {c 2}\right)
$$

2. Consider the graph with vertices corresponding to components in  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  and two components are adjacent if the total variation distance between them is at most  $1 - \varepsilon^{c_2c_3}$ . Then the induced subgraph of vertices with indices  $R_i \cup S_i$  is connected for all  $i \in [\ell]$ .

The proof of Lemma 9.9 is deferred to Section 9.1. In the following two lemmas, we then prove Theorem 9.1 for each pair  $\mathcal{M}_i, \mathcal{M}_i'$  defined in Lemma 9.9. In Lemma 9.10, we construct two mixtures of which pairs of parameters are identical or separated. We also show it suffices to work under this simplification.

Lemma 9.10. For any constant  $0 < c_{4} < 1$ , there exist  $c_{3}, c_{5}$  that depend on  $k$  and  $c_{4}$ , such that if  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}} w_{i} G_{i}$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}} w_{i}' G_{i}'$  with  $k_{1}, k_{2} \leqslant k$  and

1.  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M} + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}'$  is isotropic,  
2.  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leqslant \varepsilon$  
3.  $w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\geqslant \varepsilon^{c_{3}}$  forall  $i$

4. Let  $\mathcal{G}$  be a graph with components  $G_{i}, G_{i}^{\prime}$  in  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$  as vertex set and two components are adjacent if the total variation distance between them is at most  $1 - \varepsilon^{c_3}$ . Then  $\mathcal{G}$  is connected

then there exist two mixtures of Gaussians  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}} = \sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{k}_1} \tilde{w}_i \tilde{G}_i$ ,  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}' = \sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{k}_2} \tilde{w}_i' \tilde{G}_i'$  such that

1. Any pair in  $\{\tilde{\mu}_i\} \cup \{\tilde{\mu}_i'\}$  is either identical or separated by at least  $\varepsilon^{c_4c_5}$  
2. Any pair in  $\{\tilde{\Sigma}_i\} \cup \{\tilde{\Sigma}_i'\}$  is either identical or separated by at least  $\varepsilon^{c_4c_5}$  in Frobenius norm.  
3.  $\left\| \mathbb{E}(h_m(\tilde{\mathcal{M}})) - \mathbb{E}(h_m(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}'))\right\|_F \leqslant O_k(\varepsilon^{c_5})$  for any  $m \leqslant O(k)$  
4. There exist  $\pi_1:[k_1]\to [\tilde{k}_1]$  and  $\pi_2:[k_2]\to [\tilde{k}_2]$  such that

$$
\sum_ {i: \pi_ {1} (i) = j} w _ {i} = \tilde {w} _ {j}, \sum_ {i: \pi_ {2} (i) = j} w _ {i} ^ {\prime} = \tilde {w} _ {j} ^ {\prime},
$$

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (G _ {i}, \tilde {G} _ {\pi_ {1} (i)}) \leqslant \operatorname {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon), \quad f o r a l l i \in [ k _ {1} ]
$$

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (G _ {i} ^ {\prime}, \tilde {G} _ {\pi_ {2} (i)} ^ {\prime}) \leqslant \mathrm {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon), \quad f o r a l l i \in [ k _ {2} ].
$$

Proof. For any  $0 < c_{4} < 1$ , there is  $\ell \in [k^{2}]$  such that the distance between any pair of parameters in  $\{\mu_i\} \cup \{\mu_i'\}$  or the Frobenius distance between any pair in  $\{\Sigma_i\} \cup \{\Sigma_i'\}$  is not in the interval  $[\varepsilon^{(c_4 / 2)^{\ell - 1}}, \varepsilon^{(c_4 / 2)^{\ell}})$ .

Now consider a graph  $\mathcal{G}$  on  $k_{1} + k_{2}$  nodes where each node represents a vector in  $\{\mu_i\} \cup \{\mu_i'\}$  and two vectors  $a, b$  are adjacent if

$$
\left\| a - b \right\| \leqslant \varepsilon^ {(c _ {4} / 2) ^ {\ell - 1}}.
$$

We now construct new mixtures  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ . For each connected component in  $\mathcal{G}$  say  $\{\mu_{i_1},\ldots ,\mu_{j_1}^\prime ,\ldots \}$ , pick a representative say  $\mu_{i_1}$  and set  $\tilde{\mu}_{i_1} = \dots = \tilde{\mu}_{j_1}^{\prime} = \dots = \mu_{i_1}$ . Do this for all connected components and similar in the graph on covariance matrices with edges  $(i,j)$  if

$$
\left\| \Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma_ {j} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \varepsilon^ {(c _ {4} / 2) ^ {\ell - 1}}.
$$

After replacing close parameters with a representative, we may get some exactly same components in each new mixture. We then merge components with same means and covariances by adding their weights. Since all representatives of means and covariances are in different connected components of the graphs, they are separated by at least  $\varepsilon^{(c_4/2)^\ell}$ . Setting  $c_5 = 1/2(c_4/2)^{\ell-1}$  gives a separation of  $\varepsilon^{c_4c_5}$ .

Next we prove 3. There is a natural mapping  $\pi_1:[k_1]\to [\tilde{k}_1]$  that maps any component in  $\mathcal{M}$  to the merged component in  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  and a similar mapping  $\pi_2:[k_2]\to [\tilde{k}_2]$  for  $\mathcal{M}',\tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$ . For all  $i$ , we have

$$
\left\| \tilde {\mu} _ {\pi_ {1} (i)} - \mu_ {i} \right\|, \left\| \tilde {\mu} _ {\pi_ {2} (i)} ^ {\prime} - \mu_ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\|, \left\| \tilde {\Sigma} _ {\pi_ {1} (i)} - \Sigma_ {i} \right\| _ {F}, \left\| \tilde {\Sigma} _ {\pi_ {2} (i)} ^ {\prime} - \Sigma_ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\| _ {F} \leqslant O _ {k} (1) \varepsilon^ {(c _ {4} / 2) ^ {\ell - 1}} \tag {9.1}
$$

because for any pair of parameters above say  $\tilde{\mu}_{\pi_1(i)}$  and  $\mu_{i}$ , there is a path of length at most  $2k$  connecting  $\mu_{i}$  to the representative of the connected component, and each edge connects a pair

with TV distance at most  $\varepsilon$ . Suppose  $\left\| \mu_i \right\|$ ,  $\| \Sigma_i - I \|_F \leqslant \Delta$ . Then by Definition 2.4, we have for any integer  $m$ ,

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\mathcal {M})\right) - \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}})\right) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant O _ {k} (m) \Delta^ {m} \varepsilon^ {(c _ {4} / 2) ^ {\ell - 1}}.
$$

Since  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M} + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}'$  is isotropic and the minimum weight in  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M} + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}'$  is at least  $\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{c_3}$ , we have  $\left\| \mu_i\right\| \leqslant \sqrt{2 / \varepsilon^{c_3}}$  for all  $i$ . Applying Lemma 9.4 to  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M} + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}'$ , we have  $\| I - \Sigma_i\| _F\leqslant \mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon^{c_3})^{-1}$ . So there is a constant  $a$  such that  $\Delta \leqslant \varepsilon^{-ac_3}$ . If we take  $c_{3} > 0$  so that  $ac_{3}O(k)\leqslant 1 / 2(c_{4} / 2)^{\ell -1}$  and take  $c_{5} = 1 / 2(c_{4} / 2)^{\ell -1}$ , then

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\mathcal {M})\right) - \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}})\right) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant O _ {k} (m) \varepsilon^ {(c _ {4} / 2) ^ {\ell - 1} - O (m) a c _ {3}} = O _ {k} \left(\varepsilon^ {c _ {5}}\right)
$$

for  $m \leqslant O(k)$ . By the same argument, we have the similar inequality for  $\mathcal{M}'$  and  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\mathcal {M} ^ {\prime})) - \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}} ^ {\prime})) \right\| _ {F} = O _ {k} (\varepsilon^ {c _ {5}}).
$$

Since we can use Proposition 3.3 to robustly estimate the Hermite tensors of a Gaussian mixture with  $\varepsilon$ -fraction of noise and  $\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon)$  error guarantee, we must have

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\mathcal {M})\right) - \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} \left(\mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}\right)\right) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \operatorname {p o l y} _ {k} (\varepsilon).
$$

Then by the triangle inequality,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}})) - \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}} ^ {\prime})) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \left\| \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\mathcal {M})) - \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}})) \right\| _ {F} + \\ \| \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\mathcal {M})) - \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\mathcal {M} ^ {\prime})) \| _ {F} + \left\| \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\mathcal {M} ^ {\prime})) - \mathbb {E} (h _ {m} (\tilde {\mathcal {M}} ^ {\prime})) \right\| _ {F} = O (\varepsilon^ {c _ {5}}). \\ \end{array}
$$

For the last conclusion, from the definition of  $\pi_1$  and  $\pi_2$ , we know that

$$
\sum_ {i: \pi_ {1} (i) = j} w _ {i} = \tilde {w} _ {j}, \sum_ {i: \pi_ {2} (i) = j} w _ {i} ^ {\prime} = \tilde {w} _ {j} ^ {\prime}.
$$

Applying Lemma 9.4 to  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M} + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}'$ , we have that eigenvalues of  $\Sigma_{i}$  and  $\Sigma_{i}'$  are at least  $\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon^{c_3})$  for all  $i$ . Then if  $c_{3}$  is sufficiently small, by Lemma 9.3, (9.1) implies  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(G_i,\tilde{G}_{\pi (i)})\leqslant \mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$  and  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(G_i',\tilde{G}_{\pi (i)}')\leqslant \mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$  for all  $i$ .

The following lemma shows the identifiability under the simplification of Lemma 9.10. It is proved in the proof of Lemma 8.2 in [LM20].

Lemma 9.11. Suppose  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k_1} w_i G_i$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k_2} w_i' G_i'$  satisfies 1,2,3 in the conclusion of Lemma 9.10 with constants  $c_4, c_5$  and the minimal weights are at least  $\varepsilon^{c_3}$ . There exists a sufficiently small function  $f(k) > 0$  depending only on  $k$  such that if  $c_4 \leqslant f(k)$ , then  $k_1 = k_2$  and there exists a permutation  $\pi$  such that  $|w_i - w_{\pi(i)}'| \leqslant \mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$  and  $G_i = G_{\pi(i)}'$ .

Proof. Consider the component  $G_{k_2}' = N(\mu_{k_2}', \Sigma_{k_2}')$  in  $\mathcal{M}'$ . We claim that there must be some  $i \in [k_1]$  such that

$$
\left(\mu_ {i}, \Sigma_ {i}\right) = \left(\mu_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime}, \Sigma_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime}\right).
$$

Assume for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case. Let  $S_{1} = \{i\in [k_{1}]:\Sigma_{i} = \Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}\}$  and  $S_{2} = \{i\in [k_{2} - 1]:\Sigma_{i}^{\prime} = \Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}\}$ . Suppose  $F,F^{\prime}$  are the generating functions of  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$

$$
F = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k _ {1}} w _ {i} \exp \left(\mu_ {i} ^ {T} X + \frac {1}{2} X ^ {T} \Sigma_ {i} X y ^ {2}\right) = \sum_ {m = 0} ^ {\infty} \frac {1}{m !} h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) y ^ {n}
$$

$$
F ^ {\prime} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k _ {2}} w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \exp \left(\mu_ {i} ^ {\prime T} X + \frac {1}{2} X ^ {T} \Sigma_ {i} ^ {\prime} X y ^ {2}\right) = \sum_ {m = 0} ^ {\infty} \frac {1}{m !} h _ {m} (\mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}) y ^ {n}.
$$

Then define the differential operators

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {i} = \partial - \mu_ {i} ^ {T} X - X ^ {T} \Sigma_ {i} X y
$$

$$
\mathcal {D} _ {i} ^ {\prime} = \partial - \boldsymbol {\mu} _ {i} ^ {\prime T} \boldsymbol {X} - \boldsymbol {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} _ {i} ^ {\prime} \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {y}
$$

where partial derivatives are taken with respect to  $y$ . Now consider the differential operator

$$
\mathcal {D} = \left(\mathcal {D} _ {k _ {2} - 1} ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2 ^ {k _ {1} + k _ {2} - 2}} \dots \left(\mathcal {D} _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2 ^ {k _ {1}}} \mathcal {D} _ {k _ {1}} ^ {2 ^ {k _ {1} - 1}} \dots \mathcal {D} _ {1}
$$

By Fact 9.6,  $\mathcal{D}(F) = 0$ . By Fact 9.6 and Fact 9.7, we have

$$
\mathcal {D} (F ^ {\prime}) = P (y, X) \exp \left(\mu_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} ^ {T} X + \frac {1}{2} X ^ {T} \Sigma_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} X y ^ {2}\right)
$$

where  $P$  is a polynomial of degree

$$
\deg (P) = 2 ^ {k _ {1} + k _ {2} - 1} - 1 - \sum_ {i \in S _ {1}} 2 ^ {i - 1} - \sum_ {i \in S _ {2}} 2 ^ {k _ {1} + i - 2}
$$

with leading coefficient

$$
\begin{array}{l} C _ {0} = w _ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} \prod_ {i \in [ k _ {1} ] \backslash S _ {1}} (X ^ {T} (\Sigma_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} - \Sigma_ {i}) X) ^ {2 ^ {i - 1}} \prod_ {i \in S _ {1}} ((\mu_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} - \mu_ {i}) ^ {T} X) ^ {2 ^ {i - 1}} \\ \prod_ {i \in [ k _ {2} - 1 ] \backslash S _ {2}} (X ^ {T} (\Sigma_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} - \Sigma_ {i} ^ {\prime}) X) ^ {2 ^ {k _ {1} + i - 2}} \prod_ {i \in S _ {2}} ((\mu_ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime} - \mu_ {i} ^ {\prime}) ^ {T} X) ^ {2 ^ {k _ {1} + i - 2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

We now compare the following differentials evaluated at  $y = 0$

$$
(\mathcal {D} _ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime}) ^ {\deg (P)} \mathcal {D} (F)
$$

$$
(\mathcal {D} _ {k _ {2}} ^ {\prime}) ^ {\deg (P)} \mathcal {D} (F ^ {\prime})
$$

The first quantity is 0 because  $\mathcal{D}(F)$  is identically 0 as a formal power series. The second one is  $\Omega_k(1)C_0$ . Since for any  $i$  ( $\mu_i, \Sigma_i) \neq (\mu_{k_2}', \Sigma_{k_2}')$ , our assumptions imply that the separation between

$\mu_{i}, \mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime}$  or  $\Sigma_{i}, \Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}$  is at least  $\varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}}$ . Then we have  $C_{0} \geqslant \varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}O_{k}(1)}$  for some  $X$ . On the other hand, the coefficients of the formal power series  $F, F^{\prime}$  are the Hermite polynomials  $h_m(\mathcal{M})$  and  $h_m(\mathcal{M}')$ . This is a contradiction with our assumption that

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) - \mathbb {E} \left(h _ {m} (\mathcal {M}) \right. \right\| _ {F} \leqslant O _ {k} \left(\varepsilon^ {c _ {5}}\right) \right.
$$

as long as  $c_4$  is smaller than some sufficiently small function  $f(k)$  depending only on  $k$ . Thus there must be some component of  $\mathcal{M}$  that matches  $G_{k_2}' = N(\mu_{k_2}', \Sigma_{k_2}')$ . We can repeat the argument for each component in  $\mathcal{M}'$  and in  $\mathcal{M}$  to conclude that  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  have the same components.

Next we will show that the weights of the same components in  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  are close. We can assume that  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i' G_i$  are two mixtures on the same set of components. Without loss of generality,

$$
w _ {1} - w _ {1} ^ {\prime} \leqslant \dots w _ {\ell} - w _ {\ell} ^ {\prime} \leqslant 0 \leqslant w _ {\ell + 1} - w _ {\ell + 1} ^ {\prime} \leqslant \dots \leqslant w _ {k} - w _ {k} ^ {\prime}.
$$

Then we can consider the following two mixtures

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(w _ {1} - w _ {1} ^ {\prime}\right) G _ {1} + \dots + \left(w _ {\ell} - w _ {\ell} ^ {\prime}\right) G _ {\ell} \\ \left(w _ {\ell + 1} - w _ {\ell + 1} ^ {\prime}\right) G _ {\ell + 1} + \dots + \left(w _ {k} - w _ {k} ^ {\prime}\right) G _ {k}. \\ \end{array}
$$

If

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} | w _ {i} - w _ {i} ^ {\prime} | > \varepsilon^ {\zeta}
$$

for some sufficiently small  $\zeta$  depending only on  $k$ , we can then normalize each of the above into a distribution and repeat the same argument, using the fact that pairs of components cannot be too close, to obtain a contradiction. Thus, the mixing weights of  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  are  $\mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$ -close and this completes the proof.

Proof of Theorem 9.1. We first set  $c_4 = f(k)$  as in Lemma 9.11, and then  $c_3, c_5$  according to  $c_4$  as in Lemma 9.10, and  $c_1', c_2$  according to  $c_3$  as in Lemma 9.9. Let  $c_1 = \min \{c_1', c_2c_3\}$ .

By Lemma 9.8, we can find  $i$  such that there is no  $w_{j}, w_{j}^{\prime}$  in  $[\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i - 1}}, \varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}})$ . Let  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}} = \sum_{\{j: w_{j} \geqslant \varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}} w_{j} G_{j}$  and  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}' = \sum_{\{j: w_{j}' \geqslant \varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}} w_{j}' G_{j}'$ . Then  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}, \tilde{\mathcal{M}}') \leqslant O(\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i - 1}})$ . Let  $\varepsilon_{1} = \varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i - 1}}$ . We have  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}, \tilde{\mathcal{M}}') \leqslant O(\varepsilon_{1})$  and the minimum weights of  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}, \tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$  are at least  $\varepsilon_{1}^{c_{1}}$ .

Now we can apply Lemma 9.9 on  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$  and get partitions of components of  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$ . For  $i\in [\ell ]$ , let  $\mathcal{M}_i$  and  $\mathcal{M}_i^\prime$  be the mixtures defined in Lemma 9.9. We can apply a linear transformation to make  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}_i + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}_i^\prime$  in isotropic position. Since the total variation distance is invariant under linear transformations, so we still have both conclusions in Lemma 9.9. Let  $\varepsilon_{2} = \varepsilon_{1}^{c_{2}}$ . Then  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}_i,\mathcal{M}_{\pi (i)})\leqslant O(\varepsilon_2)$  and  $\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M} + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$  satisfies Lemma 9.4 with  $\delta = \varepsilon_2^{c_3}$ . Weights of both mixtures increase when we do the partition. So minimum weights are at least  $\varepsilon_1^{c_1}\geqslant \varepsilon_1^{c_2c_3} = \varepsilon_2^{c_3}$ .

We now prove the statement on these smaller mixtures. First we can use Lemma 9.10 to merge close parameters of  $\mathcal{M}_i, \mathcal{M}_i'$  so that all pairs of parameters are either equal or separated by  $\varepsilon_2^{c_4c_5}$ . Under this simplification, Lemma 9.11 shows that there is a perfect matching between the same

components in two mixtures and their weights are almost the same. By the last statement in Lemma 9.10, it is also a matching between components of  $\mathcal{M}_i$  and  $\mathcal{M}_i^\prime$  by combining  $\pi$  and  $\pi_1,\pi_2$ . Moreover, if  $\tilde{G}_j = \tilde{G}_{\pi (j)}'$ , then  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(G_\ell ,G_\ell^{\prime})\leqslant \mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon_2)$  for all  $\ell ,\ell '$  such that  $\pi_1(\ell) = j,\pi_2(\ell ') = \pi (j)$ . Repeating the argument for all pieces in  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}$  completes the proof.

# 9.1 Proof of Lemma 9.9

In this section, we will prove Lemma 9.9. The following fact in [Liu-Moitra] shows that a good set of clusters of one mixture exists.

Fact 9.12 (Claim 7.6 in [LM'20]). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$  be a mixture of Gaussians. For any constants  $0 < \delta < 1$  and  $\varepsilon > 0$ , there exists  $t \in [k^2]$  such that there exists a partition (possibly trivial) of  $[k]$  into sets  $R_1, \ldots, R_\ell$  such that

1. If we draw edges between all pairs  $i, j$  such that  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_i, G_j) \leqslant 1 - \varepsilon^{\delta^t}$ , then each piece of the partition is connected  
2. For any  $i, j$  in different pieces of the partition,  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_i, G_j) \geqslant 1 - \varepsilon^{\delta^{t-1}}$ .

Remark. Fact 9.12 can be applied to a set of Gaussians instead of a mixture of Gaussians by randomly assigning positive weights for all Gaussians.

Lemma 9.13. For any constant  $0 < c < 1$ , suppose  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k_1} w_i A_i$ ,  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i=1}^{k_2} w_i' B_i$  are two mixtures of arbitrary distributions with  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \mathcal{M}') \leqslant \varepsilon$  and  $w_i, w_i' \geqslant \varepsilon^c$ . If for any  $i \neq j$ ,  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_i, B_j) \geqslant 1 - \varepsilon$ , then  $k_1 = k_2$  and  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_i, B_i) \leqslant \mathrm{poly}_{k_1}(\varepsilon)$  for all  $i \in [k_1]$ .

Proof. Suppose  $\pi$  is any coupling of  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  and  $X, Y$  are random variables with distributions  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$ . Then  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \mathcal{M}') = \min_{\pi} \{\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X \neq Y)\}$ . We define  $\pi$  to be the optimal coupling such that  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}, \mathcal{M}') = \mathbb{P}_{\pi}(X \neq Y)$ . Then we can define  $\hat{\pi}$  on variables  $i, j, X, Y$  such that  $\sum_{i \in [k_1], j \in [k_2]} \hat{\pi}(i, j, X, Y) = \pi(X, Y)$  and the marginal distribution  $\hat{\pi}_X$  with fixed  $i$  of  $X$  is  $w_i A_i$  for all  $i \in [k_1]$  and the marginal distribution  $\hat{\pi}_Y$  with fixed  $j$  is  $w_j'B_j$  for all  $j \in [k_2]$ . Let  $P_{ij} = \int_{X,Y} \hat{\pi}(i, j, X, Y)dX dY$  and  $A_{ij} = \frac{1}{P_{ij}} \int_Y \hat{\pi}(i, j, X, Y)dY$  be distributions on  $X$ ,  $B_{ij} = \frac{1}{P_{ij}} \int_X \hat{\pi}(i, j, X, Y)dX$  be distributions on  $Y$ . Then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (\mathcal {M}, \mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}) = \underset {\pi} {\mathbb {P}} (X \neq Y) = \underset {\hat {\pi}} {\mathbb {P}} (X \neq Y) \\ = \sum_ {i, j} P _ {i j} \underset {\hat {\pi}} {\mathbb {P}} (X \neq Y \mid i, j) \tag {9.2} \\ \geqslant \sum_ {i, j} P _ {i j} \cdot d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i j}, B _ {i j}). \\ \end{array}
$$

By the definition of  $P_{ij},A_{ij},B_{ij}$

$$
w _ {i} A _ {i} = P _ {i j} A _ {i j} + \sum_ {j ^ {\prime} \neq j} P _ {i j ^ {\prime}} A _ {i j ^ {\prime}}
$$

$$
w _ {i} ^ {\prime} B _ {i} = P _ {i j} B _ {i j} + \sum_ {i ^ {\prime} \neq i} P _ {i ^ {\prime} j} B _ {i ^ {\prime} j}
$$

Dividing both sides by  $\max \{w_i, w_j'\}$ , we get

$$
A _ {i} = \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} A _ {i j} + \left(1 - \frac {w _ {i}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}}\right) A _ {i} + \sum_ {j ^ {\prime} \neq j} \frac {P _ {i j ^ {\prime}}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} A _ {i j ^ {\prime}}
$$

$$
B _ {i} = \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} B _ {i j} + \left(1 - \frac {w _ {j} ^ {\prime}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}}\right) B _ {i} + \sum_ {i ^ {\prime} \neq i} \frac {P _ {i ^ {\prime} j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} B _ {i ^ {\prime} j}
$$

From the above two equations, we can write  $A_{i},B_{i}$  as linear combinations of two distributions.

$$
A _ {i} = \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} A _ {i j} + \left(1 - \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}}\right) A _ {i} ^ {\prime}
$$

$$
B _ {i} = \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} B _ {i j} + \left(1 - \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}}\right) B _ {i} ^ {\prime}
$$

Then by the triangle inequality,

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i}, B _ {j}) \leqslant \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}} d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i j}, B _ {i j}) + \left(1 - \frac {P _ {i j}}{\max \{w _ {i} , w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \}}\right)
$$

$$
P _ {i j} \cdot d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(A _ {i j}, B _ {i j}\right) \geqslant P _ {i j} - \left(1 - d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(A _ {i}, B _ {j}\right)\right) \max  \left\{w _ {i}, w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \right\}. \tag {9.3}
$$

Combining (9.2) and (9.3), we have the following inequality on the TV distance between mixtures and the TV distance between components

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (\mathcal {M}, \mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}) \geqslant \sum_ {i, j} \left(P _ {i j} - \left(1 - d _ {\mathsf {T V}} \left(A _ {i}, B _ {j}\right)\right) \max  \left\{w _ {i}, w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \right\}\right). \tag {9.4}
$$

By the lower bounds on  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_i,B_j)$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \varepsilon \geqslant d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (\mathcal {M}, \mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}) \geqslant \sum_ {i, j} P _ {i j} - \sum_ {i \neq j} (1 - d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i}, B _ {j})) \max \{w _ {i}, w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \} - \sum_ {i} (1 - d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i}, B _ {i})) \max \{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \} \\ \geqslant 1 - \sum_ {i \neq j} \varepsilon - \sum_ {i} \max  \left\{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\} + \sum_ {i} \max  \left\{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\} d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(A _ {i}, B _ {i}\right) \\ \geqslant 1 - \sum_ {i \neq j} \varepsilon - \sum_ {i} \max  \left\{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \right\} + w _ {\min } d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(A _ {1}, B _ {1}\right) \tag {9.5} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $A_{1},B_{1}$  can be replaced by any  $A_{i},B_{i}$  pair. Let  $k = \max \{k_1,k_2\}$ . When  $i\neq j$  and  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_i,B_j)\geqslant 1 - \varepsilon$ , we plug it into Equation (9.4) and get

$$
\varepsilon \geqslant d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (\mathcal {M}, \mathcal {M} ^ {\prime}) \geqslant \sum_ {i \neq j} P _ {i j} - (1 - d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i}, B _ {j})) \max \{w _ {i}, w _ {j} ^ {\prime} \} \geqslant \sum_ {i \neq j} (P _ {i j} - \varepsilon).
$$

This implies

$$
\sum_ {i \neq j} P _ {i j} \leqslant k ^ {2} \varepsilon .
$$

Then we can bound  $\sum_{i}\max \{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\} -1$  in Equation (9.5)

$$
\sum_ {i} \max \{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \} - 1 = \sum_ {i} \max \{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \} - w _ {i} \leqslant \sum_ {i \neq j} P _ {i j} \leqslant k ^ {2} \varepsilon .
$$

Plugging this bound into Equation (9.5), for any  $i$ , we have

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} (A _ {i}, B _ {i}) \leqslant \frac {1}{w _ {\min}} \left(k ^ {2} \varepsilon + \sum_ {i} \max \{w _ {i}, w _ {i} ^ {\prime} \} - 1\right) \leqslant \frac {2 k ^ {2} \varepsilon}{\varepsilon^ {c}}.
$$

Proof of Lemma 9.9. We apply Fact 9.12 on the union set of components of  $\mathcal{M}$  and  $\mathcal{M}'$  with parameter  $\delta$  to find a partition  $R_1, \ldots, R_\ell$ . Let

$$
\mathcal {M} _ {i} = \frac {\sum_ {G _ {j} \in R _ {i}} w _ {j} G _ {j}}{\sum_ {G _ {j} \in R _ {i}} w _ {j}}
$$

$$
\mathcal {M} _ {i} ^ {\prime} = \frac {\sum_ {G _ {j} ^ {\prime} \in R _ {i}} w _ {j} ^ {\prime} G _ {j} ^ {\prime}}{\sum_ {G _ {j} ^ {\prime} \in R _ {i}} w _ {j} ^ {\prime}}.
$$

Then for any  $i \neq j$ , we know  $d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_a, G_b') \geqslant 1 - \varepsilon^{\delta^{t-1}}$  for  $G_a \in R_i$ ,  $G_b' \in R_j$ . By (9.4) in the proof of Lemma 9.13, we have

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} \left(M _ {i}, M _ {j} ^ {\prime}\right) \geqslant 1 - 2 k \varepsilon^ {\delta^ {t - 1}}.
$$

Then by Lemma 9.13, for any  $i$ , there exists  $a$  such that  $d_{\mathsf{T}\mathsf{V}}(M_i,M_i') \leqslant \varepsilon^{a\delta^{t - 1}}$ . Let  $c_{2} = a\delta^{t - 1}$ . If we set  $\delta = c_2c_3 / \delta^{t - 1} = ac_3$ , the partition satisfies the second conclusion.
