# A Omitted Proofs

In this subsection, we provide the proofs that were omitted from Section 2 and Section 6.

# A.1 Omitted Proofs from Section 2.1

Lemma A.1 (Concentration of low-degree polynomials, Lemma 2.9 restated). Let  $T$  be a  $d$ -dimensional, degree-4 tensor such that  $\| T \|_F \leqslant \Delta$  for some  $\Delta > 0$  and let  $x, y \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I)$ . Then, with probability at least  $1 - 1/\text{poly}(d)$ , the following holds:

$$
\left\| T (\cdot , \cdot , x, y) \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant O \left(\log (d) \Delta^ {2}\right).
$$

Proof. We note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| T (\cdot , \cdot , x, y) \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {i _ {1}, i _ {2}} \left(\sum_ {i _ {3}, i _ {4}} T (i _ {1}, i _ {2}, i _ {3}, i _ {4}) x (i _ {3}) y (i _ {4})\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {i _ {1}, i _ {2}} \left(\sum_ {i _ {3}, i _ {4}} T (i _ {1}, i _ {2}, i _ {3}, i _ {4}) ^ {2} x (i _ {3}) ^ {2} y (i _ {4}) ^ {2}\right) \right] \\ = \sum_ {i _ {1}, i _ {2}, i _ {3}, i _ {4}} T (i _ {1}, i _ {2}, i _ {3}, i _ {4}) ^ {2} \leqslant \Delta^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

The second equality follows from the fact that  $x(i_3), y(i_4)$  are independent and have zero means. So the only non-zero terms are the squares. The third equality follows from the fact that  $x(i_3), y(i_4)$  are independent with unit variances. Observe that  $\left\| T(\cdot, \cdot, x, y) \right\|_F^2$  is a degree-2 polynomial in Gaussian random variables. Using standard concentration bounds for low-degree Gaussian polynomials (see, e.g., Theorem 2.3 in [DRST14]), we obtain

$$
\mathbb {P} \left[ \left\| T (\cdot , \cdot , x, y) \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \geqslant t ^ {2} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| T (\cdot , \cdot , x, y) \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right] \right] \leqslant \exp (- c t).
$$

Setting  $t = \Omega (\log (d))$  completes the proof.

# A.2 Omitted Proofs from Section 2.2

Lemma A.2 (Spectral SoS Proofs, Lemma 2.23 restated). Let  $A$  be a  $d \times d$  matrix. Then for  $d$ -dimensional vector-valued indeterminate  $v$ , we have:

$$
\left| \frac {v}{2} \right. \left\{v ^ {\top} A v \leqslant \| A \| _ {2} \| v \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Proof. Note that  $v$  is the only variable in the proof here ( $A$  is a matrix of constants). We note that  $A \leqslant \|A\|_2 I$  or  $\|A\|_2 I - A$  is PSD and thus  $\|A\|_2 I - A = QQ^\top$  for some  $d \times d$  matrix  $Q$ . Thus,  $\|Qv\|_2^2 = v^\top (\|A\|_2 I - A)v = \|A\|_2 \|v\|_2^2 - v^\top Av$ . Thus,  $\|A\|_2 \|v\|_2^2 - v^\top Av$  is a sum of squares polynomial (namely  $\|Qv\|_2^2$ ) in variable  $v$ . This completes the proof.

![](images/b9765d318f2bc356e7ed2adc4be89cb79120c1742d61f8c6d0844a40f76c39af.jpg)

Lemma A.3 (Frobenius Norms of Products of Matrices, Lemma 2.25 restated). Let  $B$  be a  $d \times d$  matrix valued indeterminate for some  $d \in \mathbb{N}$ . Then, for any  $0 \preceq A \preceq I$ ,

$$
\left| \frac {B}{2} \right. \left\{\| A B \| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant \| B \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\},
$$

and,

$$
\left| \frac {B}{2} \right. \left\{\| B A \| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant \| B \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\},
$$

Proof. The proof of the second claim is similar so we prove only the first. We have:

$$
\left| \frac {B}{2} \right. \left\{\| B \| _ {F} ^ {2} = \| (A + I - A) B \| _ {F} ^ {2} = \| A B \| _ {F} ^ {2} + \| (I - A) B \| _ {F} ^ {2} + 2 \operatorname {t r} ((I - A) B B ^ {\top} A) \right\}
$$

Now,  $A - A^2 \succeq 0$ , thus,  $A - A^2 = RR^\top$  for some  $d \times d$  matrix  $R$ . Thus,  $\operatorname{tr}((A - A^2)BB^\top) = \operatorname{tr}(RR^\top BB^\top) = \| BR\|_F^2 - a$  sum of squares polynomial of degree 2 in indeterminate  $B$ . Thus,  $\left|\frac{B}{2}\right| \left\{\operatorname{tr}((A - A^2)BB^\top) \geqslant 0\right\}$ .

# A.3 Omitted Proofs from Section 2.3

Lemma A.4 (Shifts Cannot Decrease Variance, Lemma 2.32 restated). Let  $\mathcal{D}$  be a distribution on  $\mathbb{R}^d$ ,  $Q$  be a  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate, and  $C$  be a scalar-valued indeterminate. Then, we have that

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q , C}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (Q (x) - C) ^ {2} \right]\right\}.
$$

Proof.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q , C}{2} \right.\left\{ \right.\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (Q (x) - C) ^ {2} \right] = \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ] - C\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ]\right) ^ {2} \right] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (Q (x) - C) ^ {2} \right] \\ + 2 \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ]\right) \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ] - C\right) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ]\right) ^ {2} \right] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (Q (x) - C) ^ {2} \right] \\ \left. \geqslant \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(Q (x) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ Q (x) ]\right) ^ {2} \right] \right\}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Lemma A.5 (Shifts of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions, Lemma 2.33 restated). Let  $x$  be a mean-0 random variable with distribution  $\mathcal{D}$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  with  $t$ -certifiably  $C$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials. Then, for any fixed constant vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^d$ , the random variable  $x + c$  also has  $t$ -certifiable 4C-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials.

Proof. Observe that using that  $\mathbb{E}_{x\sim \mathcal{D}}[x] = 0$ , we have that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) \right] = \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x + c ^ {\top} Q c \right] \right\}. \right.
$$

Next, by two applications of the SoS Triangle Inequality (Fact 2.21), an application of Lemma 2.32 followed by certifiable hypercontractivity of  $\mathcal{D}$ , we have:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\left| Q \right.}{t ^ {\prime}} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left((x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] \right. \\ = \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) + x ^ {\top} Q c + c ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] \\ \leqslant 4 ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x ^ {\top} Q c) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (c ^ {\top} Q x) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right]\right) \\ \leqslant 4 ^ {t ^ {\prime}} (C t ^ {\prime}) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \right] ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x ^ {\top} Q c) ^ {2} \right] ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (c ^ {\top} Q x) ^ {2} \right] ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2}\right) \Biggr \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

On the other hand, notice that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q}{2} \right.\left\{ \right.\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left((x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \left(\mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {D}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \right] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x ^ {\top} Q c) ^ {2} \right] + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (c ^ {\top} Q x) ^ {2} \right]\right) \quad \Bigg \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus,

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{t ^ {\prime}} \right. \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} + \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x ^ {\top} Q c) ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} + \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (c ^ {\top} Q x) ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} \right.
$$

$$
\leqslant 4 ^ {t ^ {\prime}} (C t ^ {\prime}) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left((x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) \right]\right) ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} \Biggr \}.
$$

As a result, we obtain:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {| Q}{t ^ {\prime}} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left((x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] \right. \\ \leqslant \left(4 C t ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left. \left((x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ (x + c) ^ {\top} Q (x + c) \right]\right) ^ {2} \right]\right)\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} \Bigg \}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof.

Lemma A.6 (Mixtures of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions, Lemma 2.34 restated). Let  $\mathcal{D}_1,\mathcal{D}_2,\ldots ,\mathcal{D}_k$  have t-certifiable  $C$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials on  $\mathbb{R}^d$ , for some fixed constant  $C$ . Then, any mixture  $\mathcal{D} = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}$  also has t-certifiably  $(C / \alpha)$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials for  $\alpha = \min_{i\leqslant k,w_i > 0}w_i$ .

Proof. Applying Lemma 2.21 followed by SoS Hölder's inequality on the second term and followed by a final application of SoS Hölder's inequality (Fact 2.20), we obtain:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q}{t ^ {\prime}} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] = \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] \right. \right. \\ = \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} [ x ^ {\top} Q x ]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] \\ \leqslant 2 ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \right] \right. \\ \left. + \left(\sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right] \right] ^ {t ^ {\prime}}\right)\right) \\ \leqslant 2 ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\left(C t ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right]\right)\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} \\ \left. + \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \left(\mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}}\right) \\ \leqslant \left(\frac {4 C t ^ {\prime}}{\alpha}\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {t ^ {\prime} / 2} \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

On the other hand, note that by Lemma 2.32, we know that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \right.\left\{ \right.\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ x ] ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \right] = \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D}} {\mathbb {E}} [ x ^ {\top} Q x ]\right) ^ {2} \right]
$$

$$
\left. \geqslant \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] \right\}.
$$

Combining the two equations above completes the proof.

Corollary A.7 (Certifiable Hypercontractivity of  $k$ -Mixtures of Gaussians, Corollary 2.35 restated). Let  $\mathcal{D}$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians  $\sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$  with weights  $w_{i} \geqslant \alpha$  for every  $i \in [k]$ . Then,  $\mathcal{D}$  has  $t$ -certifiably  $4 / \alpha$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials.

Proof. From [KOTZ14], we know that the standard Gaussian random variable has  $t$ -certifiably 1-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials. From Fact 2.31, we immediately obtain that for any PSD matrix  $\Sigma$ , the Gaussian  $\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)$  also has  $t$ -certifiable 1-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials. From Lemma 2.33, we obtain that for any  $\mu$ , the Gaussian  $\mathcal{N}(\mu ,\Sigma)$  has  $t$ -certifiable 4-hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials. Finally, applying Lemma 2.34 to  $\mathcal{D}_i = \mathcal{N}(\mu_i,\Sigma_i)$  and mixture weights  $w_{1},w_{2},\ldots w_{k}$ , yields that  $\mathcal{D} = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$  has  $t$ -certifiably  $4 / \alpha$ -hypercontractive degree-2 polynomials. This completes the proof.

Lemma A.8 (Linear Transformations of Certifiably Bounded-Variance Distributions, Lemma 2.39 restated). For  $d \in \mathbb{N}$ , let  $x$  be a random variable with distribution  $\mathcal{D}$  on  $\mathbb{R}^d$  such that for  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate  $Q$ ,  $\left|\frac{Q}{2}\right| \left\{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}}(x^\top Qx - \mathbb{E}_\mathcal{D} x^\top Qx)\right|^2 \leqslant \left\|\Sigma^{1/2} Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\|_F^2$ . Let  $A$  be an arbitrary  $d \times d$  matrix and let  $x' = Ax$  be the random variable with covariance  $\Sigma' = AA^\top$ . Then, we have that

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\mathbb {E} _ {x ^ {\prime} \sim \mathcal {D} ^ {\prime}} \left(x ^ {\prime \top} Q x ^ {\prime} - \mathbb {E} _ {\mathcal {D} ^ {\prime}} x ^ {\prime \top} Q x ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} \leqslant \left\| \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Proof. The covariance of  $x'$  is  $AA^{\top} = \Sigma'$ , say. Let  $\Sigma'^{1/2}$  be the PSD square root of  $\Sigma'$ . The proof follows by noting that  $x'^{\top}Qx' = (Ax)^{\top}Q(Ax) = x^{\top}(A^{\top}QA)x^{\top}$  and that  $\| A^{\top}QA\|_{F}^{2} = \mathrm{tr}(A^{\top}QAA^{\top}QA) = \mathrm{tr}(AA^{\top}QAA^{\top}Q) = \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma'Q\Sigma'Q) = \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma'^{1/2}Q\Sigma'^{1/2}\Sigma'^{1/2}Q\Sigma'^{1/2}) = \left\| \Sigma'^{1/2}Q\Sigma'^{1/2}\right\|_{F}^{2}$ .

Lemma A.9 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Standard Gaussians, Lemma 2.40 restated). We have that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \leqslant 3 \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right.
$$

Proof. We will view  $xx^{\top}$  and  $I \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$  as  $d^2$ -dimensional vectors. Consider the matrix  $\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,I)}(xx^{\top} - I)(xx^{\top} - I)^{\top}$ . The diagonal of this matrix is  $2I_{d^2}$ . The off-diagonal part has exactly one non-zero entry in any row (which corresponds to entry indexed by  $(i,j)$  and  $(j,i)$  for  $i \neq j$ ), and thus has spectral norm at most 1 by the Gershgorin circle theorem. Thus,  $\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,I)}(xx^{\top} - I)(xx^{\top} - I)^{\top} \preceq 3I_{d^2}$ .

We thus have:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} = \underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left\langle x x ^ {\top} - I, Q \right\rangle^ {2} = \underset {\mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left\langle x x ^ {\top} - I, Q \right\rangle^ {2} \right. \right. \\ \leqslant \left\| \underset {x \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} (x x ^ {\top} - I) (x x ^ {\top} - I ^ {\prime}) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant 3 \| I _ {d ^ {2}} \| _ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} = 3 \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \Bigg \}. \quad (\mathrm {A . 1}) \\ \end{array}
$$

Lemma A.10 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Mixtures, Lemma 2.43 restated). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{D}_{i}$  be a  $k$ -mixture of distributions  $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \ldots, \mathcal{D}_k$  with means  $\mu_{i}$  and covariances  $\Sigma_{i}$ . Let  $\mu = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mu_{i}$  be the mean of  $\mathcal{M}$ . Suppose that each of  $\mathcal{D}_1, \mathcal{D}_2, \ldots, \mathcal{D}_k$  have certifiably  $C$ -bounded-variance i.e. for  $Q$ : a symmetric  $d \times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate.

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x ^ {\prime} \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} (x ^ {\prime^ {\top}} Q x ^ {\prime} - \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\prime^ {\top}} Q x ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} \leqslant C \left\| \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {\prime 1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Further, suppose that for some  $H > 1$ ,  $\left\| \mu_{i} - \mu \right\|_{2}^{2}$ ,  $\left\| \Sigma_{i} - I \right\|_{F} \leqslant H$  for every  $1 \leqslant i \leqslant k$ . Then, we have that

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant 1 0 0 C H ^ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Proof. We have the following sequence of (in-)equalities:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q}{2} \right\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} = \sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} (A.2) \\ = \sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x + \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} (A.3) \\ \leqslant 2 \sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} + 2 \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} \}, (A.4) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third line follows from Fact 2.21 (SoS Almost Triangle Inequality).

Let us first bound the 2nd term in the RHS above. Towards that, let  $\Sigma = \sum_{i} w_{i}((\mu_{i} - \mu)(\mu_{i} - \mu)^{\top} + \Sigma_{i})$  be the covariance of the mixture  $\mathcal{M}$ . Then, notice that  $\Sigma = \sum_{i} w_{i}((\mu_{i} - \mu)(\mu_{i} - \mu)^{\top} + \Sigma_{i}) = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mu_{i} \mu_{i}^{\top} + \sum_{i} w_{i} \Sigma_{i} - \mu \mu^{\top}$ . Thus, we can write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} + \Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma - \mu \mu^ {\top} = \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} - \mu_ {j} \mu_ {j} ^ {\top}) + \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma_ {j}) \\ = \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\mu_ {j} - \mu) (\mu_ {j} - \mu) ^ {\top} - \sum_ {j \neq i} (\mu - \mu_ {j}) (\mu - \mu_ {j}) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma_ {j}) \\ = \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\mu_ {j} - \mu) (\mu_ {j} - \mu) ^ {\top} - \sum_ {j \neq i} (\mu - \mu_ {j}) (\mu - \mu_ {j}) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\Sigma_ {i} - I) - \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} (\Sigma_ {j} - I). \\ \end{array}
$$

Here, in the second to last step, we added and subtracted  $\sum_{j\neq i}w_j\mu \mu^\top$  and used that  $\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i} = \mu$  and in the last step we added and subtracted  $\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}I$ .

By application of the triangle inequality for Frobenius norm to the RHS of the above, we have that:

$$
\left\| \mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} + \Sigma_ {i} - \mu \mu^ {\top} - \Sigma \right\| _ {F} \leqslant \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} \left\| (\mu_ {i} - \mu) (\mu_ {i} - \mu) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {F} + \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} \left\| (\mu_ {j} - \mu) (\mu_ {j} - \mu) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {F}
$$

$$
+ \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} \left\| (\Sigma_ {i} - I) \right\| _ {F} + \sum_ {j \neq i} w _ {j} \left\| (I - \Sigma_ {j}) \right\| _ {F} \leqslant H + H + H + H = 4 H.
$$

Using the SoS version of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (Fact 2.20) on indeterminate  $Q$  and constant  $\mu \mu^{\top} - \mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top} + \Sigma_{i} - \Sigma$  and the above bound, we have:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q}{2} \right\rbrace \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \left(\underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} = \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \left(\left\langle \mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} + \Sigma_ {i}, Q \right\rangle - \left\langle \mu \mu^ {\top} + \Sigma , Q \right\rangle\right) ^ {2} \\ \leqslant \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \left\| \mu \mu^ {\top} - \mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} + \Sigma_ {i} - \Sigma \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant 1 6 H ^ {2} \sum_ {i} w _ {i} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} = 1 6 H ^ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \Bigg \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Let us now bound the first term in the RHS of (A.4) above. First, observe that  $x^{\top}Qx - \mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}(\mu_i,\Sigma_i)}x^{\top}Qx = (x - \mu_i)^{\top}Q(x - \mu_i) - \mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}(\mu_i,\Sigma_i)}(x - \mu_i)^{\top}Q(x - \mu_i) + 2(x - \mu_i)^{\top}Q\mu_i$ . Thus, using Fact 2.21 and Lemma 2.42, we have:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {Q}{2} \right\{\sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} x ^ {\top} Q x\right) ^ {2} (A.5) \\ \leqslant 2 \sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left((x - \mu_ {i}) ^ {\top} Q (x - \mu_ {i}) - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} (x - \mu_ {i}) ^ {\top} Q (x - \mu_ {i})\right) ^ {2} + 8 \sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left((x - \mu_ {i}) ^ {\top} Q \mu_ {i}\right) ^ {2} (A.6) \\ \leqslant 6 \sum_ {i} w _ {i} C \left\| \Sigma_ {i} ^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma_ {i} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} + 8 \sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(\left(x - \mu_ {i}\right) ^ {\top} Q \mu_ {i}\right) ^ {2} \Bigg \}. (A.7) \\ \end{array}
$$

For the first term, note that  $\| \Sigma_i\| _2\leqslant 1 + \| \Sigma_i - I\| _F\leqslant 1 + H$  . Thus,  $\left\| \Sigma_i^{1 / 2}\right\| _2\leqslant \sqrt{1 + H}$  . Thus, we have. that  $\Sigma_{i}^{1 / 2}\preceq I + (\Sigma_{i}^{1 / 2} - I)\preceq \sqrt{1 + H} I$  . Using Lemma 2.25 with  $A = (1 + H)^{-1 / 2}\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}$  and  $B = Q\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}$  we have:  $\begin{array}{r}\left|\frac{Q}{2}\left\{\left\| \Sigma_i^{1 / 2}Q\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}\right\| _F^2\leqslant (1 + H)\left\| Q\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}\right\| _F^2\right\} . \end{array}$  By another application of Lemma 2.25, we have:  $\begin{array}{r}\left|\frac{Q}{2}\left\{\left\| Q\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}\right\| _F^2\leqslant (1 + H)\left\| Q\right\| _F^2\right\} . \end{array}$  Thus, altogether, we have:  $\begin{array}{r}\left|\frac{Q}{2}\left\{\left\| \Sigma_i^{1 / 2}Q\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}\right\| _F^2\leqslant (1 + H)^2\left\| Q\right\| _F^2\right\} . \end{array}$  Using our assumption that  $1 <   H$  , we thus have:

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\sum_ {i} w _ {i} C \left\| \Sigma_ {i} ^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma_ {i} ^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant C (1 + H) ^ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant 4 C H ^ {2} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right.
$$

For the second term, first observe that the following equality of quadratic polynomials in indeterminate  $Q$ :  $\left((x - \mu_i)^\top Q\mu_i\right)^2 = \left((\Sigma_i^{\dagger /2}(x - \mu_i))^{\top}\Sigma_i^{1 / 2}Q\mu_i\right)^2$ . Thus,  $\mathbb{E}_{x\sim \mathcal{D}_i}\left((x - \mu_i)^\top Q\mu_i\right)^2 = \left\| \Sigma_i^{1 / 2}Q\mu_i\right\| _2^2$ . Next, by the SoS Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (Fact 2.20), we have that

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \right. \left\{\left\| \Sigma_ {i} ^ {1 / 2} Q \mu_ {i} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \mathrm {t r} (\mu_ {i} \mu_ {i} ^ {\top} Q \Sigma_ {i} Q) \leqslant H \mathrm {t r} (Q \Sigma_ {i} Q) = H \left\| \Sigma_ {i} ^ {1 / 2} Q \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Applying Lemma 2.25 with the observation above that  $\Sigma_i^{1/2} \leqslant (1 + H)^{1/2} I$  yields:  $\left\{\left\|\Sigma_i^{1/2} Q\right\|_F^2 \leqslant (1 + H) \|Q\|_F^2\right\}$ . Thus, altogether, we obtain:  $\left|\frac{Q}{2}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{D}_i}(x - \mu_i)^\top Q \mu_i\right\}^2 \leqslant H(1 + H)^2\right\|Q\|_F^2 \leqslant 4H^3\|Q\|_F^2$ . We thus have:

$$
\left| \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\sum_ {i \leqslant k} w _ {i} \underset {\mathcal {D} _ {i}} {\mathbb {E}} \left((x - \mu_ {i}) ^ {\top} Q \mu_ {i}\right) ^ {2} \leqslant 4 H ^ {3} \| Q \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}. \right.
$$

Plugging in these bounds into (A.7) completes the proof.

![](images/79b09cc449b861533734059de2a88640f9b635c9aacbb2f2a800dac12ea0e5a1.jpg)

As an immediate corollary of Lemma 2.39 and Lemma 2.43, we obtain:

Lemma A.11 (Variance of Degree-2 Polynomials of Mixtures of Gaussians, Lemma 2.44 restated). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$  be a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians with  $w_{i}\geqslant \alpha$ , mean  $\mu = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}$  and covariance  $\Sigma = \sum_{i}w_{i}((\mu_{i} - \mu)(\mu_{i} - \mu)^{\top} + \Sigma_{i})$ . Suppose that for some  $H > 1$ ,  $\left\| \Sigma^{\dagger /2}(\Sigma_i - I)\Sigma^{\dagger /2}\right\| _F\leqslant H$  for every  $1\leqslant i\leqslant k$ . Let  $Q$  be a symmetric  $d\times d$  matrix-valued indeterminate. Then for  $H^{\prime} = \max \{H,1 / \alpha \}$ ,

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant 1 0 0 H ^ {\prime 2} \left\| \Sigma^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Proof. Let  $\Sigma = U\Lambda U^{\top}$  be the covariance of the mixture  $\mathcal{M}$  along with its eigendecomposition. We want to apply Lemma 2.43 and Lemma 2.39 with the linear transformation  $x \to Ax$  for  $A = \Lambda^{\dagger/2}U^{\top}$ . For this, we need to check that the conditions of the Lemma 2.43 are met after this linear transformation. The new component covariance is  $\Sigma_i' = A\Sigma_iA^{\top}$  and the hypothesis implies that they are within  $H$  in Frobenius distance of the new mixture covariance  $I' = A\Sigma A^{\top}$  ( $I$  in the range space of  $\Sigma$ ). The new means of the components after the linear transformation are  $\mu_i' = A\mu_i$  and the new mixture mean is  $\mu' = A\mu$ . Thus, noting that  $I' = \sum_{i} w_i (\mu_i' - \mu') (\mu' - \mu')^{\top} + \sum_{i} w_i \Sigma_i'$ , and since each of the terms in the RHS of the preceding equality are PSD, we must have that  $I' \geq w_i (\mu_i' - \mu') (\mu_i' - \mu')^{\top}$  for every  $i$ . Thus,  $1 = \|I'\|_2 \geqslant w_i \left\| (\mu_i' - \mu') (\mu_i' - \mu')^{\top} \right\|_2 = \left\| \mu_i' - \mu' \right\|_2^2$ . Rearranging yields that  $\left\| \mu_i' - \mu' \right\|_2^2 \leqslant 1 / w_i \leqslant 1 / \alpha$ . Thus, we can now apply Lemma 2.43 to the linearly transformed mixture and the conclusion follows.

# A.4 Omitted Proofs from Section 2.4

Lemma A.12 (Lemma 2.49 restated). If  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})$  with parameters  $(\gamma ,t)$ , then if  $w_{i}\geqslant \gamma$  for all  $i\in [k]$ , and if for some  $B\geqslant 0$  we have that  $\left\| \mu_i\right\| _2^2,\left\| \Sigma_i\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant B$  for all  $i\in [k]$ , then for all  $m\leqslant t$ , we have that:

$$
\left\| \underset {x \in_ {U} X} {\mathbb {E}} [ x ^ {\otimes m} ] - \underset {x \sim M} {\mathbb {E}} [ x ^ {\otimes m} ] \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant \gamma^ {2} m ^ {O (m)} B ^ {m} d ^ {m}.
$$

Proof. We begin by noting that for any symmetric  $m$ -tensor  $A$  we have that  $\| A \|_F^2 \leqslant m^{O(m)} (\mathbb{E}_{v \sim \mathcal{N}(0,I)}[\langle A, v^{\otimes m} \rangle^2])$ . This is because, in the notation of [DKS18], the squared expectation of  $\langle A, v^{\otimes m} \rangle$  is  $\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Hom}_A(v)^2] \geqslant m!\mathbb{E}[h_A(v)^2] = m!\|A\|_F^2$ , where the first inequality holds

because  $\sqrt{m!} h_A(v)$  is the degree- $m$  harmonic part of  $\mathrm{Hom}_A(v)$ , and the equality is by Claim 3.22. Therefore, to prove the lemma, it suffices to bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \underset {v \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\underset {x \in_ {u} X} {\mathbb {E}} [ (v \cdot x) ^ {m} ] - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} [ (v \cdot x) ^ {m} ]\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \underset {v \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {x \in X _ {i}} (v \cdot x) ^ {m} - w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu_ {i}, \Sigma_ {i})} {\mathbb {E}} (v \cdot x) ^ {m}\right)\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ = \underset {v \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {m} \binom {m} {j} \mu_ {i} ^ {m - j} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {x \in X _ {i}} (v \cdot (x - \mu_ {i})) ^ {j} - w _ {i} \underset {x \sim \mathcal {N} (\mu_ {i}, \Sigma_ {i})} {\mathbb {E}} (v \cdot (x - \mu_ {i})) ^ {j}\right)\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ \leqslant \mathbb {E} _ {v \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} \left[ \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {m} {\binom {m} {j}} | v \cdot \mu_ {i} | ^ {m - j} \left(w _ {i} \gamma m! (v ^ {T} \Sigma v) ^ {j / 2}\right)\right) ^ {2} \right] \\ \leqslant \gamma^ {2} m ^ {O (m)} \underset {v \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \left(w _ {i} (| v \cdot \mu_ {i} | + (v ^ {T} \Sigma v) ^ {1 / 2}) ^ {2 m}\right) \right] \\ \leqslant \gamma^ {2} m ^ {O (m)} \underset {v \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I)} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ 2 B ^ {m} \| v \| _ {2} ^ {2 m} \right] \\ \leqslant \gamma^ {2} m ^ {O (m)} B ^ {m} d ^ {m}. \\ \end{array}
$$

This completes the proof.

Lemma A.13 (Lemma 2.50 restated). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i} w_{i} \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$ . Let  $S \subset [k]$  with  $\sum_{i \in S} w_{i} = w$ , and let  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i \in S} (w_{i} / w) \mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}, \Sigma_{i})$ . Then if  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$  for some  $\gamma < 1/(2k)$  with the corresponding partition being  $X = X_{1} \cup X_{2} \cup \ldots \cup X_{k}$ , then  $X' = \bigcup_{i \in S} X_{i}$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}'$  with parameters  $(O(k\gamma / w), t)$ .

Proof. After noting that  $|X'| = w|X|(1 + O(k\gamma / w))$ , the rest follows straightforwardly from the definitions using the partition  $X' = \bigcup_{i \in S} X_i$ .

Lemma A.14 (Lemma 2.51 restated). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \mathcal{N}(\mu_i, \Sigma_i)$  and let  $n$  be an integer at least  $kt^{Ct} d^t / \gamma^3$ , for a sufficiently large universal constant  $C > 0$ , some  $\gamma > 0$ , and some  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ . If  $X$  consists of  $n$  i.i.d. samples from  $\mathcal{M}$ , then  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$  with high probability.

Proof. We will show that Condition 2.47 holds with high probability using that partition where  $X_{i}$  is the set of samples drawn from the  $i$ -th component of  $\mathcal{M}$ . Note that the second part of Condition 2.47 holds with high probability, so long as  $n$  is a sufficiently large multiple of  $d / \gamma^2$  by the VC-Theorem [DL01]. In particular, if we think of samples as being drawn from  $\mathbb{R}^d \times [k]$ , where the second coordinate denotes the component that the sample was drawn from, the second part of Condition 2.47 says that the empirical probability of any event  $H \times \{i\}$  is correct to within additive error  $\gamma$ . It is easy to see and well-known that the class of such events has VC-dimension  $O(d)$ , from which the desired bound follows.

For the first part of Condition 2.47, we claim that it holds with high probability so long as  $n \geqslant kt^{Ct}d^t /\gamma^3$ . To prove this, we show it separately for each  $i$  with  $w_{i} \geqslant \gamma$  (as otherwise there is nothing to prove) and take a union bound. As Condition 2.47 is invariant under affine transformations, we may perform an invertible affine transformation so that  $\mu_{i} = 0$  and  $\Sigma_{i}$  is the projection onto the first  $d^{\prime}$  coordinates, for some  $d^{\prime}$ . It is clear that only the first  $d^{\prime}$  coordinates of any element of  $X_{i}$  will be non-zero. We claim that the first part of our condition will follow for a given  $m$ , so long as  $||X_i| / n - w_i| \leqslant \gamma w_i$  (which holds with high probability if  $n \gg \log (k) / \gamma^3$ ), and

$$
\left\| \mathbb {E} _ {x \in_ {u} X _ {i}} [ x ^ {\otimes m} ] - \mathbb {E} _ {x \sim \mathcal {N} (0, I _ {d ^ {\prime}})} [ x ^ {\otimes m} ] \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \leqslant \gamma^ {2}, \tag {A.8}
$$

as  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in X_i}\langle v,x - \mu_i\rangle^m = w_i(1\pm \gamma)\langle \mathbb{E}_{x\in_uX_i}[x^{\otimes m}],v^{\otimes m}\rangle$  . It is easy to see that each entry of the tensor on the left hand side of Equation (A.8) has mean 0 and variance  $m^{O(m)} / |X_i|$ , and thus the expected size of the left hand side is  $m^{O(m)}d^{m} / |X_{i}|$ . Then, when  $n\geqslant k^{Ck}d^{4k} / \gamma^3$  for a sufficiently large constant  $C$ , all parts of our condition hold with high probability. This completes the proof.

# A.5 Omitted Proofs from Section 6

Lemma A.15 (Frobenius Distance to TV Distance, Lemma 6.5, restated). Suppose  $\mathcal{N}(\mu_1,\Sigma_1),\mathcal{N}(\mu_2,\Sigma_2)$  are Gaussians with  $\left\| \mu_1 - \mu_2\right\| _2\leqslant \delta$  and  $\| \Sigma_1 - \Sigma_2\| _F\leqslant \delta$ . If the eigenvalues of  $\Sigma_{1}$  and  $\Sigma_{2}$  are at least  $\lambda >0$  then

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\mathcal {N} (\mu_ {1}, \Sigma_ {1}), \mathcal {N} (\mu_ {2}, \Sigma_ {2})) = O (\delta / \lambda).
$$

Proof. By Fact 2.1, we have

$$
d _ {\mathsf {T V}} \left(\mathcal {N} (\mu_ {1}, \Sigma_ {1}), \mathcal {N} (\mu_ {2}, \Sigma_ {2})\right) = O \Big (\big ((\mu_ {1} - \mu_ {2}) ^ {\top} \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1} (\mu_ {1} - \mu_ {2}) \big) ^ {1 / 2} + \| \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} \Sigma_ {2} \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} - I \| _ {F}) \Big).
$$

Then the first term is  $\left\langle \mu_1 - \mu_2, \Sigma_1^{-1}(\mu_1 - \mu_2) \right\rangle^{1/2} \leqslant \left(\left\| \Sigma_1^{-1} \right\|_{\mathrm{op}} \left\| \mu_1 - \mu_2 \right\|_2^2\right)^{1/2} \leqslant \delta / \sqrt{\lambda}$ . The second term is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} \Sigma_ {2} \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} - I \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} = \left\| \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\Sigma_ {1} - \Sigma_ {2}) \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2} \left(\Sigma_ {1} - \Sigma_ {2}\right) \Sigma_ {1} ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \leqslant \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\Sigma_ {1} - \Sigma_ {2}\right) ^ {2}\right) (1 / \lambda) ^ {2} \\ \leqslant (\delta / \lambda) ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus,

$$
d _ {\mathrm {T V}} (\mathcal {N} (\mu_ {1}, \Sigma_ {1}), \mathcal {N} (\mu_ {2}, \Sigma_ {2})) = O (\delta / \sqrt {\lambda} + \delta / \lambda) = O (\delta / \lambda).
$$

![](images/a6fcdee430203100be03c601978175e2960f1ba6b3c6893ffa03b327362044d9.jpg)

Lemma A.16 (Component Moments to Mixture Moments, Lemma 6.6 restated). Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i\in [k]}w_i\mathcal{N}(\mu_i,\Sigma_i)$  be a  $k$ -mixture such that  $w_{i}\geqslant \alpha$ , for some  $0 < \alpha < 1$ , and  $\mathcal{M}$  has mean  $\mu$  and covariance

$\Sigma$  and for all  $i \neq j \in [k]$ ,  $\left\| \Sigma^{\dagger/2} \left( \Sigma_{i} - \Sigma_{j} \right) \Sigma^{\dagger/2} \right\|_{F} \leqslant 1 / \sqrt{\alpha}$ . Let  $X$  be a multiset of  $n$  samples satisfying Condition 2.47 with respect to  $\mathcal{M}$  with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$ , for  $0 < \gamma < (dk / \alpha)^{-ct}$ , for a sufficiently large constant  $c$ , and  $t \in \mathbb{N}$ . Let  $\mathcal{D}$  be the uniform distribution over  $X$ . Then,  $\mathcal{D}$  is  $2t$ -certifiably  $(c / \alpha)$ -hypercontractive and for  $d \times d$ -matrix-valued indeterminate  $Q$ ,  $\left|\frac{Q}{2} \left\{ \mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}}(x^{\top} Q x - \mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}} x^{\top} Q x)^{2} \leqslant O(1 / \alpha) \left\| \Sigma^{1/2} Q \Sigma^{1/2} \right\|_{F}^{2} \right\}\right.$ .

Proof. First, since  $\mathcal{M}$  is a  $k$ -mixture of Gaussians with minimum mixing weight  $w_{\min} \geqslant \alpha$ , it follows from Corollary 2.35 that  $\mathcal{M}$  is  $t$ -certifiably  $(4 / \alpha)$  hypercontractive. Further, since  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$ , it follows from Lemma 2.48 that the set  $X' = \{\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(x_i - \mu)\}_{x_i \in X}$  also satisfies Condition 2.47 with parameters  $(\gamma, t)$  w.r.t.  $\mathcal{M}' = \sum_{i \in [k]} w_i \mathcal{N}(\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\mu_i - \mu), \Sigma^{\dagger/2} \Sigma_i \Sigma^{\dagger/2})$ . Since  $\left\| \Sigma^{\dagger/2} \Sigma_i \Sigma^{\dagger/2} \right\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leqslant O(1 / \alpha)$ , it follows from Lemma 2.49 that for all  $m \leqslant t$ ,  $\| \mathbb{E}_{x \in_{u} X'}[x^{\otimes m}] - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim M'}[x^{\otimes m}] \|_F^2 \leqslant \gamma^2 m^{O(m)} d^m (1 / \alpha)^m$ . Since  $\gamma < (dk / \alpha)^{-O(t)}$ , it follows from Fact 2.45 that  $X$  is  $2t$ -certifiably  $(c / \alpha)$ -hypercontractive.

By assumption, for all  $i \neq j \in [k]$ , we have that  $\left\| \Sigma^{\dagger / 2} \left( \Sigma_{i} - \Sigma_{j} \right) \Sigma^{\dagger / 2} \right\|_{F} \leqslant 1 / \sqrt{\alpha}$ . We can now apply Lemma 2.44 to obtain

$$
\left| \right. \frac {Q}{2} \left\{\underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[\left(x ^ {\top} Q x - \underset {x \sim \mathcal {M}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ x ^ {\top} Q x \right]\right) ^ {2} \right] \leqslant O (1 / \alpha) \| \Sigma^ {1 / 2} Q \Sigma^ {1 / 2} \| _ {F} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

Therefore, it follows from Fact 2.45 that since  $X$  satisfies Condition 2.47 with parameters  $(\gamma ,t)$ , the uniform distribution  $\mathcal{D}_X$  on  $X,\left|\frac{Q}{2}\right.$ $\left\{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim \mathcal{D}_X}(x^\top Qx - \mathbb{E}_{x\sim \mathcal{D}_X}x^\top Qx)^2\leqslant O(1 / \alpha)\left\| \Sigma^{1 / 2}Q\Sigma^{1 / 2}\right\| _F^2\right\}$ .

# B Bit Complexity Analysis

Here we address numerical issues related to our computation. We begin with the assumption that the eigenvalues of our covariance matrices are bounded below.

Lemma B.1. If  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$  is a mixture of Gaussians  $G_i$  where each  $G_i$  has mean and covariance of norm at most  $2^b$  for some positive integer  $b$  and each  $G_i$  has covariance matrix whose eigenvalues are bounded below by some  $\lambda > 0$ . Let  $\mathcal{M}'$  be an  $\varepsilon$ -corruption of  $\mathcal{M}$  whose outputs are bounded by  $2^{O(b)}$ . Let  $N$  be a sufficiently large polynomial in  $d^k / \varepsilon$  and let  $\eta$  be  $\lambda$  divided by a sufficiently large polynomial in  $2^b d / \varepsilon$  (where sufficiently large is degree  $O(1)$ ). Then if our algorithm is given  $N$  i.i.d. samples from  $\mathcal{M}'$  with each of their coordinates rounded to a nearby multiple of  $\eta$  (by which we mean one of the two closest), then our algorithm runs in time  $\mathrm{poly}(N, b, \log(1 / \eta))$  and with high probability returns a list of mixtures of Gaussians  $X_i$  with at least one of the  $X_i$  poly $_k(\varepsilon)$ -close to  $\mathcal{M}$  in parameter distance.

Proof. This follows from noting firstly that with high probability the any subset of the rounded samples will have moments  $\lambda / \mathrm{poly}(d / \varepsilon)$ -close to their moments before rounding. This means that with high probability these rounded samples will satisfy Condition 2.47. This means that our algorithm satisfies the necessary correctness guarantees. Furthermore, given that our samples now all have bounded bit complexity, it is easy to see that the runtime of our algorithm is polynomial in  $N$  and the bit complexity.

More generally, as long as the parameters of the components of our mixture can be expressed with bounded bit complexity, we can prove a similar result, without needing any lower bound on the covariances.

Theorem B.2. Let  $\mathcal{M} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i G_i$  be a mixture of Gaussians where the  $G_i$  are Gaussians whose means and covariance matrices can all be written with coefficients given by rational numbers with bit complexity at most  $b$  for some integer  $b$ . Let  $\mathcal{M}'$  be an  $\varepsilon$ -corruption of  $\mathcal{M}$  so that with probability 1 the returned points have size  $2^{O(b)}$ . Let  $N$  be a sufficiently large polynomial in  $d^k / \varepsilon$ . Then there exists an algorithm that given  $b$  bit-oracle access to these samples runs in time  $\mathrm{poly}(N, b)$  and with high probability returns a mixture of Gaussians  $X$  so that  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(X, \mathcal{M}) < \mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$ .

Proof. We begin by showing that we can find a list of hypotheses at least one of which is close. It is then straightforward to show that we can run a tournament over these hypotheses to find a specific one that works. We also assume for simplicity that each  $w_{i}$  is at least  $3\varepsilon$ .

We begin by setting  $\lambda$  to be  $2^{-b\cdot d^{kC}}$  for a sufficiently large constant  $C$ . By adding each sample to a random sample from  $N(0,\lambda I)$ , we can produce samples from  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$ , and  $\varepsilon$ -corruption of  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}} = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i \tilde{G}_i$  where  $\tilde{G}_i$  is the convolution of  $G_i$  with  $N(0,\lambda I)$ . Note that  $\tilde{G}_i$  is a Gaussian whose covariance has eigenvalues at least  $\lambda$ . Furthermore, if the covariance matrix of  $G_i$  is non-singular, the smallest eigenvalue of the covariance matrix must be at least  $2^{O(b\cdot d)}$ , and therefore  $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(G_i, \tilde{G}_i) < \varepsilon$ .

Since the eigenvalues of the components of  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  are bounded below, we can apply Lemma B.1 to our samples from  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}'$  rounded to an appropriate accuracy  $\eta$ , and in  $\mathrm{poly}(N,b)$ -time obtain a list of hypothesis mixtures at least one of which is (with high probability) close to  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  in total variation distance.

If the covariances of all of the  $G_{i}$  with weights more than some sufficiently large  $\mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$  are all non-singular, then one of these hypotheses will be close to  $\mathcal{M}$ . Otherwise, there must be some  $i$  for which  $w_{i}$  is relatively large and for which  $G_{i}$  has singular covariance matrix. In particular, there must be an integer vector  $v$  with bit complexity  $O(bd)$  in the kernel of the covariance matrix of  $G_{i}$ . The hypothesis mixture  $X$  that is close to  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  in parameter distance must contain some component close to  $\tilde{G}_{i}$ . Since  $\tilde{G}_{i}$  has covariance matrix  $\tilde{\Sigma}_{i} = \lambda I + \Sigma_{i}$  where  $\Sigma_{i}$  is the covariance matrix of  $G_{i}$ . We note that  $\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}$  will have an eigenvalue of  $\lambda$  and that therefore, our close hypothesis will have an eigenvalue at most  $2\lambda$ .

If any of our returned hypotheses have any component with a covariance matrix  $\Sigma$  which has any eigenvalue less than  $2\lambda$ , we do the following. We consider the quadratic form on integer vectors  $v$  defined by

$$
Q (v) = v ^ {T} \Sigma v + \sqrt {\lambda} | v | _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

We note that if this  $\Sigma$  is close in parameter distance to a singular  $\tilde{\Sigma}_i$  where  $\Sigma_i$  had a null-vector  $v$  of norm  $2^{O(bd)}$ , then for that same value of  $v$  we will have that  $Q(v) < \lambda^{1/4}$ . Using the Lovász local lemma in [EL73], we can find a  $v$  so that  $Q(v)$  is within a  $2^{O(d)}$ -factor of the minimum possible value over all non-zero, integer vectors  $v$ . If for this  $v$ ,  $Q(v) > 2^{\Omega(d)}\lambda^{1/4}$ , we know that the hypothesis in question is not close to  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  in parameter distance and can be ignored. On the other hand, any  $v$  with  $Q(v)$  this small must have  $|v| < 2^{O(d)}\lambda^{-1/2}$  and  $v^T\Sigma v < 2^{O(d)}\lambda^{1/4}$ . Note that the projection of  $v$  onto the  $\ker(\Sigma_i)^\perp$  is either zero or has magnitude at least  $2^{O(bd)}$ . In the latter case, it would need to be the

case that  $Q(v)$  is substantially larger. Thus, if such a hypothesis is close to  $\tilde{\mathcal{M}}$  in parameter distance, then  $v$  is in the kernel of some  $\Sigma_{i}$ .

If our algorithm finds some  $v$  for some hypothesis, we then compute  $v \cdot x$  to error  $\lambda$  for each of our samples  $x$ . If  $\mathcal{M}$  really has a component with  $v$  in the kernel of its covariance matrix, all of the  $x$ 's taken from this component will have  $v \cdot x$  the same. This means that at least a  $(3/2)\varepsilon$  fraction of our samples  $x$  will have  $v \cdot x$  within  $\lambda$  of each other. Note that if  $v$  is not in the kernel of any covariance matrix of any  $G_i$  than  $\mathbf{Var}[v \cdot G_i]$  will be at least  $2^{O(db)}$  for each  $i$ , and with high probability we will not find this many close samples.

To summarize, if our algorithm applies this procedure to every component of every hypothesis and does not find such a  $v$ , then it cannot be the case that  $\mathcal{M}$  contains any components of weight more than  $\mathrm{poly}_k(\varepsilon)$  that are singular, and thus one of our original hypotheses must be close in total variational distance. We can then run a tournament to find a single one that is close. Otherwise, if we find such a  $v$  for which many points do have  $v \cdot x$  close by, then  $v$  must be a null vector of the covariance matrix of some  $G_i$ . Furthermore, all of the samples within  $\lambda$  of this common value of  $v \cdot x$ , with high probability are either errors or come from components contained in some lower dimensional subspace. We can determine what this subspace is by noting that it is defined by  $v \cdot x = q$  for some rational number  $q$  with bit-complexity at most  $O(bc)$  and using continued fractions on a good numerical approximation of  $q$  in order to determine its true value. Our algorithm can then recurse on the points in this subspace (a mixture of Gaussians in a lower dimensional space) and on the remaining points (which are from a mixture of fewer Gaussians), and return an appropriate mixture of the results.
